is a geodesic arc-segment due to (
3.5
). It follows that
\langle\widehat{y}-x,v_{z}\rangle<0
, since
\widehat{y}\notin\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
. But now a computation analogous to that presented in the proof of Lemma
2.9
(ii) leads to the contradiction
\left\lVert\widehat{y}-x\right\rVert<\varepsilon
.

(ii) Assume then that condition (b) is satisfied. Now
z_{n}\notin E_{\varepsilon}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
so that
v_{z}\in\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
. In addition, given that either (
3.2
) or (
3.3
) is satisfied for each
z_{n}
and (
3.3
) implies
z_{n}\in E_{\varepsilon}
, condition (
3.2
) necessarily holds true for all
z_{n}
.
Then
\xi_{1}\neq-\xi_{2}
, since
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
leads to the contradiction

z_{n}\in B_{1/n}(x)\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\{1,2\}}\overline{U_{1/n}(x,\xi_{i})}=B_{1/n}(x)\setminus B_{1/n}(x)=\varnothing.

On the other hand, if
\xi_{1}\neq-\xi_{2}
, Proposition
2.12
states that
v_{z}\in\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
can be written as a convex combination
v_{z}=a\xi_{1}+b\xi_{2}
. Note that at least one of the coefficients
a,b
must be strictly positive, since
v_{z}\in S^{1}
. However, (
3.2
) implies
\langle z_{n}-x,\xi_{i}\rangle<0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which leads to the contradiction
\langle v_{z},\xi_{i}\rangle\leq 0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
∎

3.2.
Approximating the Boundary with Continuous Graphs

In order to study the properties of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, we utilise a finite approximation scheme as a technical aid. The idea is to generate a non-decreasing sequence
(D^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of finite subsets
D^{n}\subset E
and use their
\varepsilon
-boundaries
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(D^{n})
to approximate the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
in small neighbourhoods around individual boundary points
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact, let
\varepsilon>0
and let
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large so that
2^{-n_{0}}<\varepsilon/4
. Consider for natural numbers
n>n_{0}
the partitions of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
into squares

(3.6)

\mathcal{C}^{n}:=\left\{C_{k,\ell}^{n}:=\left[\frac{k}{2^{n}},\frac{k+1}{2^{n}}\right]\times\left[\frac{\ell}{2^{n}},\frac{\ell+1}{2^{n}}\right]:k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

It follows from the axiom of choice that there exists a non-decreasing sequence
D^{n_{0}}\subset D^{n_{0}+1}\subset\dots
of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, in which the sets
D^{n}
are defined for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
by the property

(3.7)

D^{n}\cap C_{k,\ell}^{n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad\text{if}\,\,}l}\big\{d_{k,\ell}^{n}\big\},&E\cap C_{k,\ell}^{n}\not=\emptyset,\\
\emptyset,&E\cap C_{k,\ell}^{n}=\emptyset,\end{array}\right.

where the points
d_{k,\ell}^{n}
are chosen arbitrarily from
E\cap C_{k,\ell}^{n}
.

Figure 3.3
.
Schematic illustration of two consecutive finite approximating sets for the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
around a wedge
x
. (
A
) The boundaries
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(d_{k,\ell}^{n})
for
d_{k,\ell}^{n}\in D^{n}
(red dots) provide an approximation (red curve) for
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. (
B
) For
D^{n+1}
the approximation improves, and the sets
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(D^{n})\cap B_{r}(x)
converge to
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x)
in Hausdroff distance as
n\to\infty
.

Definition 3.2

(Finite approximating sets)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and let
(D^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of subsets
D^{n}\subset E
as defined in (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) above. The sets
D^{n}
are then called
finite approximating sets
for the set
E
.

It is immediate from the definition of the sets
D^{n}
that

E_{\varepsilon}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq n_{0}}B_{\varepsilon}(D^{n})}
. In fact, the sets
B_{\varepsilon}(D^{n})
converge to
E_{\varepsilon}
in Hausdroff distance.
1
1

1

The
Hausdorff distance

\mathrm{dist}_{H}(X,Y)
between two sets
X,Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined as
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(X,Y):=\mathrm{inf}\{\delta>0\,:\,X\subset Y_{\delta}\,\,\,\textrm{\emph{and}}\,\,\,Y\subset X_{\delta}\}
,
where
X_{\delta}:=\overline{\bigcup_{x\in X}B_{\delta}(x)}
.

However, one needs to exercise some caution because the boundaries
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(D^{n})
do not in general converge to
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
in Hausdorff distance. This can be seen for instance by considering the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods of finite approximating sets for the example given in Figure
1.3
in Section
1.1
.

We now show that the sequence of boundaries
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(D^{n})
can nevertheless be used to approximate the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
locally around individual boundary points
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. The local contribution property, Proposition
3.1
, implies that in order to describe the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
near any
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, one only needs to consider the boundaries of
\varepsilon
-balls centered at contributors that lie close to the extremal contributors
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
. To make this precise, we establish a local coordinate system that allows us to represent the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
locally as a union of function graphs.

Definition 3.3

(Extremal pairs)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be closed, let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and denote by
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
the sets of extremal outward directions and extremal contributors, respectively. The set of
extremal pairs
at
x
is the collection

\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon}):=\big\{(\xi,y)\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})\times\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)\,:\,\langle x-y,\xi\rangle=0\big\}.

The idea of extremal pairs is that the vectors
\xi
and
(x-y)/\varepsilon
serve as an orthonormal basis for a local coordinate system, aligned with the local boundary geometry.
Now, let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, and consider for each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
the sets

(3.8)

E^{\xi,y}:=E\cap U_{\varepsilon/2}(y,\xi),\quad D_{n}^{\xi,y}:=D^{n}\cap U_{\varepsilon/2}(y,\xi),

where
D^{n}
is a finite approximating set for
E
and
U_{\varepsilon/2}(y,\xi)
is a half-ball of radius
\varepsilon/2
centered at
y
, oriented towards
\xi
, see (
3.1
). Using the sets
D_{n}^{\xi,y}
, we may now define a sequence of functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}:[0,\varepsilon/2]\to\mathbb{R}
by setting

(3.9)

f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s):=\textrm{max}\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\mathrm{dist}\left(x+s\xi+t\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon},D_{n}^{\xi,y}\right)\leq\varepsilon\right\},

where
\textrm{dist}\left(\cdot,D_{n}^{\xi,y}\right)
denotes the Euclidean distance from the set
D_{n}^{\xi,y}
. Since
\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\big(D_{n}^{\xi,y}\big)}
is compact, the maximum in (
3.9
) exists for all
s\in[0,\varepsilon/2]
. Compactness furthermore implies that the functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}
are bounded for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition, it follows from the definition of the functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)
that

(3.10)

x+s\xi+f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)(x-y)\varepsilon^{-1}\in\partial\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\big(D_{n}^{\xi,y}\big)}

for all
s\in[0,\varepsilon/2]
. Since for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the boundary
\partial\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\big(D_{n}^{\xi,y}\big)}
is composed of a finite collection of arc-segments, the corresponding functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}
are continuous.

Lemma 3.4

(Convergence of approximating functions)
.

Let
E
be compact and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then, for each
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}

and each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
,
the functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}
defined in (
3.9
), converge monotonically and uniformly to the continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,\varepsilon/2]\to\mathbb{R}
, given by

(3.11)

f^{\xi,y}(s):=\mathrm{max}\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,\mathrm{dist}\left(x+s\xi+t\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon},E^{\xi,y}\right)\leq\varepsilon\right\}

as
n\to\infty
. Furthermore,

(3.12)

\big\{x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\varepsilon^{-1}(x-y)\,:\,s\in[0,\varepsilon/2]\big\}=\partial\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(E_{\xi,y})}\cap U_{\varepsilon/2}(x,\xi),

where
E_{\xi,y}
is as in (
3.8
).

Proof.

Let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. Since the sets
D_{n}^{\xi,y}
form a non-decreasing sequence, it is clear that
f_{n+1}^{\xi,y}(s)\geq f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)
for all
s\in[0,\varepsilon/2]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. On the other hand, the value
f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)
is bounded from above by

Hence, the functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}
converge to a limiting function pointwise and monotonically on
[0,\varepsilon/2]
. We now argue that the convergence is uniform and that
\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)=f^{\xi,y}(s)
for all
s\in[0,\varepsilon/2]
.

Let
\delta>0
and consider a point
x_{s}:=f^{\xi,y}(s)
for some
s\in[0,\varepsilon/2]
. Then
x_{s}\in\partial\overline{B_{\varepsilon/2}(E^{\xi,y})}
by (
3.11
). Let
y\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
be an extremal contributor of
x_{s}
. It follows from the definition of the finite approximating sets that there exists for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
some
y_{n}\in D_{n}^{\xi,y}
for which
\left\lVert y-y_{n}\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2}/n
.
Denote by
\textrm{proj}_{y}(x_{s},\partial B_{\varepsilon}(y_{n}))
the projection of
x_{s}
onto
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(y_{n})
along the
x-y
axis. Then, since the difference in the
\xi
-coordinate between
x_{s}
and
y_{n}
is less than
\varepsilon/2
, we furthermore have

\displaystyle\big|f^{\xi,y}(s)-f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)\big|

\displaystyle<\big|f^{\xi,y}(s)-\textrm{proj}_{y}(x_{s},\partial B_{\varepsilon}(y_{n}))\big|

\displaystyle<\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\big(\left\lVert x_{s}-y_{n}\right\rVert-\varepsilon\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\left\lVert y-y_{n}\right\rVert\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}n},

so that
|f^{\xi,y}(s)-f_{n}^{\xi,y}(s)|<\delta
whenever
n>2\sqrt{2}/\sqrt{3}\delta
. Since this estimate is independent of
s
, it follows that
f_{n}^{\xi,y}\to f^{\xi,y}
uniformly, as claimed. The last claim follows directly from the definition of
f^{\xi,y}
in (
3.11
).
∎

We now apply Lemma
3.4
to obtain local representations for the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
around each boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. The proof of Proposition
3.5
below makes use of Lemma
3.8
which we have placed in the subsequent Section
3.3
, together with other results on the geometry of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
.

Proposition 3.5

(Local boundary representation)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be closed and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. For each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
there exists a continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,\varepsilon/2]\to\mathbb{R}
and a corresponding function
g_{\xi,y}:[0,\varepsilon/2]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, given by

(3.13)

g_{\xi,y}(s):=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon},

so that the collection of functions
\mathcal{G}(x):=\big\{g_{\xi,y}\,:\,(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})\big\}
satisfies

(3.14)

\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}=\bigcup_{(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})}g_{\xi,y}\left(A_{\xi,y}\right)

for some
r>0
and some closed
A_{\xi,y}\subset[0,\varepsilon/2]
. We call the collection
\mathcal{G}(x)
a
local boundary representation (of radius
r
)
at
x
. For each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
the corresponding subset
A_{\xi,y}\subset[0,\varepsilon/2]
is either

(a)

an interval
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
for some
0<s_{\xi,y}\leq\varepsilon/2
, or

(b)

a closed set whose complement in
[0,\varepsilon/2]
contains a sequence of disjoint open intervals with
0
as an accumulation point.

For wedges (singularities of type S1) and
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, case (a) above holds true for all
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

Figure 3.4
.
Schematic illustration of the first functions in the sequence
\left(f_{n}^{\xi,y}\right)_{n=1}^{\infty}
. The red dots in (
A
) and (
B
) represent the points in two consecutive finite approximating sets
D_{1}^{\xi,y}
and
D_{2}^{\xi,y}
, respectively.

Proof.

Consider an extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and let
f^{\xi,y}:[0,\varepsilon/2]\to\mathbb{R}
be as in (
3.11
). Define
g_{\xi,y}:[0,\varepsilon/2]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

(3.15)

g_{\xi,y}(s):=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}.

Substituting
\delta=\varepsilon/2
in Proposition
3.1
guarantees the existence of some
r>0
, for which
z\in E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x)
if and only if either

\textrm{(i) }z\notin\overline{U_{r}(x,\xi)}\textrm{ for each }\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon}),\quad\textrm{or}\quad\textrm{(ii) }z\in\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\left(E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x))\right)}.

According to Proposition
2.14
, the set of tangential directions
T_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})

coincides with the set of extremal outward directions
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. This implies that condition (i) necessarily fails for all boundary points
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x)
. Therefore, condition (ii) holds true whenever
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x)
, and in this case
z\in\partial\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(E_{\xi,y})}\cap\overline{U_{r}(x,\xi)}
for some
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. On the other hand, according to Lemma
3.4
we have

(3.16)

g_{\xi,y}([0,\varepsilon/2])=\partial\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(E_{\xi,y})}\cap U_{\varepsilon/2}(x,\xi),

see (
3.12
). Hence, there exists some
s_{z}\in[0,r]
, for which
z=g_{\xi,y}(s_{z})
. In other words, every boundary point
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
inside the neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
lies on a graph
g_{\xi,y}([0,r])
for some
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

It remains to be verified, for each
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, which arguments
s\in[0,r]
satisfy
g_{\xi,y}(s)\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x)
. We divide the rest of the proof into two cases, depending on whether or not there exist extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
for which

(3.17)

y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x).

Condition (
3.17
) is schematically illustrated in Figure
4.1
(
C
), in the beginning of Chapter
4
.

(i) Assume (
3.17
) is not satisfied by any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
. Then there is either only one contributor so that
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, or else
x
is a wedge, see Definition
4.1
. In both cases we have
\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{(\xi_{1},y_{1}),(\xi_{2},y_{2})\}
for some
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi_{\textrm{ext}}(x)
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
, where we allow for
y_{1}=y_{2}
in case
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
. It follows from Lemma
3.8
below that for any outward directions
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})_{S^{1}}
there exists some radius
\rho
and a corresponding neighbourhood
B_{\rho}(x)\subset B_{r}(x)
in which the graphs of
g_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}
and
g_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}
are separated by the cone
V_{\rho}(x,\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
, see (
3.21
). Hence

(3.18)

g_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}([0,r)\cap g_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}([0,r])\cap B_{\rho}(x)=\varnothing.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
we define the upper bounds

s_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}:=\max\big\{s\in[0,r]\,:\,g_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}\left([0,s]\right)\in\overline{B_{\rho}(x)}\big\}

and the corresponding sets
A_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}:=\left[0,s_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}\right]
. It follows then from (
3.18
) and Proposition
3.1
that
g_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}(A_{\xi_{i},y_{i}})\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, which implies

\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{\rho}(x)}=\bigcup_{i\in\{1,2\}}g_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}\left(A_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}\right).

(ii) Assume then that (
3.17
) holds true for
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)

and consider some
\xi\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
. In this case the graphs
g_{\xi,y_{i}}([0,s])
,
i\in\{1,2\}
may generally intersect for arbitrarily small
s\in(0,r]
.
Due to (
3.17
) one can write for
\{i,j\}=\{1,2\}

\displaystyle g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)

\displaystyle=x+s\xi+\left(f^{\xi,y_{j}}(s)-\alpha_{\xi}(s)\right)\frac{x-y_{j}}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=g_{\xi,y_{j}}(s)-\alpha_{\xi}(s)\frac{x-y_{j}}{\varepsilon},

where
\alpha_{\xi}(s):=f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)
. Hence it follows for
i\in\{1,2\}
from Proposition
3.1
and the definitions of the functions
f_{n}^{\xi,y}
and
f^{\xi,y}
in (
3.9
) and (
3.11
) that

\displaystyle g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)

\displaystyle\notin\partial E_{\varepsilon}\,\,\mathrm{whenever}\,\,\alpha_{\xi}(s)>0,\,\mathrm{and}

\displaystyle g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)

\displaystyle\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\,\,\mathrm{whenever}\,\,\alpha_{\xi}(s)<0.

For
\alpha_{\xi}(s)=0
one has
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
if and only if there exists a sequence
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
s_{n}\to s
, for which
\alpha_{\xi}(s_{n})<0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This follows from the fact that

\tau g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+(1-\tau)g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}

whenever
s\in(0,r)
,
\alpha_{\xi}(s)<0
and
\tau\in(0,1)
. Equation (
3.14
) is therefore satisfied for the neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
and the sets

\displaystyle A_{\xi,y_{i}}:=A_{\xi}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\left\{s\in[0,r]\,:\,\alpha_{\xi}(s)<0\right\}}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=\left\{s\in[0,r]\,:\,g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s),g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\right\},

where
i\in\{1,2\}
and
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.
Now either
[0,s_{\xi}]\subset A_{\xi}
for some
s_{\xi}\in(0,r]
or otherwise
[0,T]\setminus A_{\xi}\neq\varnothing
for all
T>0
. In either case
0
is an accumulation point of
A_{\xi}
, since
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and
\xi\in T_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
, see Proposition
2.14
.
∎

Consider a boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and the corresponding local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
with radius
r>0
, and let
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x)
with

z=g_{\xi,y}(s_{z})=x+s_{z}\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s_{z})\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}

for some
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and
s_{z}\in[0,r]
. The construction given in Proposition
3.5

guarantees that, when written in the
(\xi,y)
-coordinates, the
\xi
-coordinate
s_{w}
of each contributor
w\in\Pi_{E}(z)
satisfies
0\leq s_{w}\leq r\leq\varepsilon/2
. This implies a lower and upper bound for the local growth-rate of the function
f^{\xi,y}
.
Combining this observation with Proposition
2.14

allows us to deduce that the functions
f^{\xi,y}
in (
3.13
) are in fact Lipschitz continuous on
[0,r]
for all
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

Proposition 3.6

(Local boundary representation is Lipschitz)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
. Then, for each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, the function
f^{\xi,y}
in (
3.13
) is
1/\sqrt{3}
-Lipschitz, and the function
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
is
2/\sqrt{3}
-Lipschitz on the interval
[0,r]
.

Proof.

Figure 3.5
.
For all
s\in(0,r)\subset(0,\varepsilon/2)
, the slopes
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)
are bounded from above by the ratio
D_{\mathrm{max}}=a/(\varepsilon/2)=(\varepsilon/2)/b=1/\sqrt{3}
.
Similarly, a lower bound is given by
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)\geq D_{\mathrm{min}}=-1/\sqrt{3}
.

Assume contrary to the claim that for some
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
there exist some
p>1/\sqrt{3}
and
s,w\in[0,r]
for which
|f^{\xi,y}(s)-f^{\xi,y}(w)|\geq p|s-w|
. We present the argument for the case
w<s
and
f^{\xi,y}(s)-f^{\xi,y}(w)\geq p(s-w)
. Assuming
f^{\xi,y}(s)-f^{\xi,y}(w)\leq-p(s-w)
leads to a contradiction through similar reasoning.
Write
m:=(s+w)/2
and define

(w_{1},s_{1}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c@{,\quad\text{if}\,\,\,}l}(m,s)&f^{\xi,y}(s)-f^{\xi,y}(m)>p(s-m)\\
(w,m)&f^{\xi,y}(s)-f^{\xi,y}(m)\leq p(s-m)\end{array}\right.

so that
f^{\xi,y}(s_{1})-f^{\xi,y}(w_{1})\geq p(s_{1}-w_{1})
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define inductively
m_{n}:=(s_{n}+w_{n})/2
and

(w_{n+1},s_{n+1}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c@{,\quad\text{if}\,\,\,}l}(m_{n},s_{n})&f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})-f^{\xi,y}(m_{n})>p(s_{n}-m_{n})\\
(w_{n},m_{n})&f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})-f^{\xi,y}(m_{n})\leq p(s_{n}-m_{n})\end{array}\right.

so that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

(3.19)

f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})-f^{\xi,y}(w_{n})\geq p(s_{n}-w_{n}).

By construction, the sequence
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is non-increasing, and the sequence
(w_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
non-decreasing. Since
|s_{n}-w_{n}|=2^{-n}|s-w|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and
s_{n}>w_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a unique limit
a=\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}w_{n}
. From (
3.19
) it follows that

(3.20)

p\leq D_{n}:=k_{s}(n)\frac{f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})-f^{\xi,y}(a)}{s_{n}-a}+k_{r}(n)\frac{f^{\xi,y}(a)-f^{\xi,y}(w_{n})}{a-w_{n}},

where the coefficients

k_{s}(n):=\frac{s_{n}-a}{s_{n}-w_{n}},\quad k_{w}(n):=\frac{a-w_{n}}{s_{n}-w_{n}}

satisfy
k_{s}(n)+k_{w}(n)=1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In case
s_{N}=a
(resp.
w_{N}=a
) for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the coefficient
k_{s}(n)
(resp.
k_{w}(n)
) vanishes for all
n\geq N
. Hence at least one and possibly both of the right and left derivatives of
f^{\xi,y}
at
a
are given by

\displaystyle D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(a)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})-f^{\xi,y}(a)}{s_{n}-a},

\displaystyle D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(a)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{f^{\xi,y}(a)-f^{\xi,y}(w_{n})}{a-w_{n}}

and correspond to the tangential directions on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
at

x(a):=x+a\xi+f^{\xi,y}(a)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}.

According to Proposition
2.14
these in turn coincide with the extremal outward directions
\xi_{a}^{+}
and
\xi_{a}^{-}
at
x(a)
. Note that the
\xi
-coordinates of the corresponding extremal contributors
y_{a}^{+},y_{a}^{-}
lie on the interval
[0,\varepsilon/2]
, see Figure
3.5
. Thus, the directional derivatives necessarily satisfy the upper bound

D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(a)\leq\frac{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\big(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\big)^{2}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}.

The contradiction
p\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}D_{n}=1/\sqrt{3}<p
then follows by taking the limit
n\to\infty
in (
3.20
).

It follows from the above reasoning that for all
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and each
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, the functions
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
are
2/\sqrt{3}
-Lipschitz, since for any
w,s\in[0,r]

\displaystyle\|g_{\xi,y}(s)-g_{\xi,y}(w)\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{|s-w|^{2}+|f^{\xi,y}(s)-f^{\xi,y}(w)|^{2}}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}|s-w|.\qed

Since a Lipschitz-function is differentiable almost everywhere, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.7

(Local boundary representation is differentiable almost everywhere)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
. For each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, the function
f^{\xi,y}
in (
3.13
) is differentiable almost everywhere.

3.3.
Local Structure of the Complement

In this section we analyse the connectedness of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
near points
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
and wedges. For these points, Lemma
3.9
guarantees the existence of a unique connected component
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which
x\in\partial V
, while Proposition
3.10
makes the stronger statement that in fact
B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap B_{r}(x)
for some connected set
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
and a neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
.

Lemma 3.8

(Approximations of the outward cone)
.

Let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\textrm{\emph{int}}_{S^{1}}\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
and define for each
r>0
the truncated cone

(3.21)

V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\left\{x+sv\,:\,v\in(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})_{S^{1}},\,0<s<r\right\}.

Then there exists
r>0
for which

V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Assume to the contrary that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
z_{n}\in E_{\varepsilon}\cap V_{1/n}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
. Then
z_{n}\to x
as
n\to\infty
and we may assume without loss of generality that

\frac{z_{n}-x}{\left\lVert z_{n}-x\right\rVert}\to\xi\in[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]_{S^{1}}.

Since
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\textrm{int}_{S^{1}}\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
, Proposition
2.12
implies
\xi\in\textrm{int}_{S^{1}}\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
, which together with Lemma
2.10
(i) gives
\langle y-x,\xi\rangle<0
for all
y\in\Pi_{E}(x)
. It follows then from Lemma
2.9
(ii) that there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
, for which
z_{n}\notin E_{\varepsilon}
for all
n\geq N
, which contradicts the assumption.
∎

Lemma 3.9

(Unique connected component)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
either be a wedge (type S1) or
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
. Then there exists a unique connected component
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, for which
x\in\partial V
.

Proof.

Since
\textrm{int}_{S^{1}}\Xi(x)\neq\varnothing
whenever
x
is a wedge or
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, there exist outward directions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\textrm{int}_{S^{1}}\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
. Lemma
3.8
then guarantees the existence of some
r>0
for which the truncated open cone

(3.22)

V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\left\{x+sv\,:\,v\in(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})_{S^{1}},\,0<s<r\right\}

satisfies
V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Consequently, there exists a connected component
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
that satisfies
V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\subset V
and
x\in\partial V
.

For each
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, let
f^{\xi,y}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
be the continuous function corresponding to the local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
with radius
0<r<\varepsilon/2
, see (
3.13
). To prove uniqueness, assume contrary to the claim that there exists another connected component
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
with
W\neq V
and
x\in\partial W
. Then for at least one extremal outward direction
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
there exists a sequence
\big(t_{n}^{W}\big)_{n=0}^{\infty}
of coefficients and some
N\in\mathbb{N}
for which

w(n):=x+n^{-1}\xi+t_{n}^{W}\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}\in W

and
t_{n}^{W}>f^{\xi,y}(n^{-1})
for all
n>N
. Note that the outward directions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
in the definition of the cone
V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
above may be chosen arbitrarily close to the extremal outward directions. Hence, it follows that there exist some outward directions
\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}
, a corresponding radius
r^{\prime}>0
and
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for all
n>N^{\prime}
there exist coefficients
t_{n}^{V}>f^{\xi,y}(n^{-1})
satisfying

(3.23)

v(n):=x+n^{-1}\xi+t_{n}^{V}\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}\in V_{r^{\prime}}(x,\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}).

Since
V_{r^{\prime}}(x,\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime})\cap V_{r}(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\neq\varnothing
, (
3.23
) implies
v(n)\in V
for all
n>N^{\prime}
. It follows then from the definition of the local boundary representation that

z(n,\alpha):=x+n^{-1}\xi+\big(\alpha t_{n}^{W}+(1-\alpha)t_{n}^{V}\big)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}\notin E_{\varepsilon}

for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
. But then the line segment from
z(n,0)
to
z(n,1)
connects the sets
W
and
V
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, which contradicts the assumption that
V
and
W
are disjoint connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

Proposition 3.10

(Geometry of the complement)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
either be a wedge or
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
. Then there exists some
r>0
, for which

(3.24)

B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap B_{r}(x)=\bigcup_{0<\rho<r}x+A(\rho),

where
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
is connected and for each
\rho\in(0,r)
either
A(\rho)=\rho(\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{\rho})_{S^{1}}
or
A(\rho)=\rho\big(S^{1}\setminus[\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{\rho}]_{S^{1}}\big)
for
\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{\rho}\in S^{1}
and
\alpha_{\rho}\to\xi_{\alpha}
,
\beta_{\rho}\to\xi_{\beta}
, where
\Xi_{\textrm{ext}}(x)=\{\xi_{\alpha},\xi_{\beta}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
, see Proposition
3.5
. Then each
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
is of the form

g_{\xi,y}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}

where the functions
f^{\xi,y}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous. We divide the proof into two parts according to whether (i)
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
or (ii)
x
is a wedge.

(i) Assume first that
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
with
\Pi_{E}(x)=\{y\}
. Then
\mathcal{G}(x)=\left\{g_{\xi_{1},y},g_{\xi_{2},y}\right\}
where
g_{\xi_{i},y}\,:\,[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and

g_{\xi_{i},y}(s)=x+s\xi_{i}+f^{\xi_{i},y}(s)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Consider for each
s\in(0,r)
the distances

\mathcal{D}_{1}(s):=\left\lVert g_{\xi_{1},y}(s)-x\right\rVert,\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}(s):=\left\lVert g_{\xi_{2},y}(s)-x\right\rVert.

Due to Proposition
2.14
and Lemma
2.13
(i) we can assume
r
to be small enough so that both
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
are strictly increasing on
(0,r)
.
One can thus define for each
\rho\in(0,r)
the points
\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{\rho}\in S^{1}
by

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}:=\frac{g_{\xi_{1},y}\left(\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-1}(\rho)\right)-x}{\rho},\qquad\beta_{\rho}:=\frac{g_{\xi_{2},y}\left(\mathcal{D}_{2}^{-1}(\rho)\right)-x}{\rho}.

According to Lemma
3.9
there exists a unique connected component
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which
x\in\partial V
. Due to Proposition
3.1
, we can assume
r
to be sufficiently small so that

B_{r}(x)\cap E\subset\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\left(E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(y)\right)}.

This implies that for each
\rho\in(0,r)
the geodesic curve segment

A(\rho):=\rho(\alpha_{\rho},(x-y)/\varepsilon)_{S^{1}}\cup\{\rho(x-y)/\varepsilon\}\cup\rho((x-y)/\varepsilon,\beta_{\rho})_{S^{1}}\subset\rho S^{1}

satisfies

x+A(\rho)\subset V\cap B_{r}(x)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad x+\rho S^{1}\setminus A(\rho)\subset\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\left(E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(y)\right)}\subset E_{\varepsilon}.

Hence,

B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap B_{r}(x)=\bigcup_{0<\rho<r}x+A(\rho).

(ii) Let then
x
be a wedge. As above, one can assume that the distances

\mathcal{D}_{1}(s):=\left\lVert g_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}(s)-x\right\rVert,\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}(s):=\left\lVert g_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}(s)-x\right\rVert

are strictly increasing in
(0,r)
. Furthermore, since
\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\rangle>-1
, one may define the average
\xi_{\mathrm{av}}:=(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})/\left\lVert\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\right\rVert
. Due to Lemma
2.13
(i) and the fact that
\xi_{\mathrm{av}}\notin\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, we may assume
r
to be small enough so that the boundary segments represented by the functions
g_{\xi_{i},y_{i}}
are separated by the line segment
\{x+\rho\xi_{\mathrm{av}}\,:\,\rho\in(0,r)\}
in the neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
.

For each
\rho\in(0,r)
, we once again define the points
\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{\rho}\in S^{1}
by

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}:=\frac{g_{\xi_{1},y}\left(\mathcal{D}_{1}^{-1}(\rho)\right)-x}{\rho}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\beta_{\rho}:=\frac{g_{\xi_{2},y}\left(\mathcal{D}_{2}^{-1}(\rho)\right)-x}{\rho}.

Analogously to the reasoning in the case
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, Proposition
3.1
and Lemma
3.9
guarantee that for all
\rho\in(0,r)
the geodesic curve segment

\widehat{A}(\rho):=\rho(\alpha_{\rho},\xi_{\mathrm{av}})_{S^{1}}\cup\{\rho\xi_{\mathrm{av}}\}\cup\rho(\xi_{\mathrm{av}},\beta_{\rho})_{S^{1}}\subset\rho S^{1}

satisfies

x+\widehat{A}(\rho)\subset V\cap B_{r}(x)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad x+\rho S^{1}\setminus\widehat{A}(\rho)\subset\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\left(E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(\{y_{1},y_{2}\})\right)}\subset E_{\epsilon},

where
V
is the unique connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which
B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=B_{r}(x)\cap V
. Hence

B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap B_{r}(x)=\bigcup_{0<\rho<r}x+\widehat{A}(\rho).\qed

Chapter 4
Classification of Boundary Points

In this chapter we present a classification of the boundary points
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
based on their local geometric and topological properties. Using the results obtained in Chapters
2
and
3
above, we prove our first main result, Theorem
1
, which states that the classification given in Definition
4.1
defines a partition of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
into disjoint subsets.

The geometric aspect of the classification scheme relies on the orientation of the extremal contributors
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
at each boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. In the planar case, there are essentially three different ways this orientation can be realised, depicted schematically in Figure
4.1
below. The defining property
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
for the extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
in case (c) can be equivalently expressed by
\big\langle(y_{1}-x)/\varepsilon,(y_{2}-x)/\varepsilon\big\rangle=-1
,
and we will make use of both formulations in what follows. Significantly, it follows from (
1.6
) and
[
Fu85
, Lemma 4.2]
that
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is a critical point of the distance function
d_{E}
if and only if it is of type (
C
) in Figure
4.1
.

Figure 4.1
.
Three basic scenarios for local boundary geometry in terms of the relative positions of the extremal contributors. In our classification of boundary points (Definition
4.1
), case (
A
) corresponds to smooth points and singularity types S4 and S5, case (
B
) to type S1, and case (
C
) to types S2, S3 and S6–S8. (
A
) At each
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
the extremal outward directions satisfy
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
, and the set
\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
spans a half-circle. (
B
) Each wedge
x
has two extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}
and two extremal outward directions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
, forming an angle
\theta=\sphericalangle(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
. (
C
) For
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
with
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
, the set of extremal outward directions satisfies
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})\subset\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}
.

4.1.
Types of Singularities

Definition
4.1
below provides a classification of all possible boundary singularities into eight categories. For a schematic illustration of the different types of singularities, see Figure
1.1
in the Introduction. Recall that
U_{r}(x,v)
denotes an open
x
-centered half-ball of radius
r
, oriented in the direction of
v\in S^{1}
, see (
3.1
). We denote by
S(E_{\varepsilon})
the set of singularities on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
.

Definition 4.1

(Types of singularities)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be closed, let
x\in S(E_{\varepsilon})
and let
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}
be the set of extremal outward directions, where we allow for the possibility
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
. We define the following eight types of singularities.

S1:

x
is a
wedge
,
if
\xi_{1}\notin\{\xi_{2},-\xi_{2}\}
, i.e.
0<\theta<\pi
for the angle
\theta
between the vectors
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
;

S2:

x
is a
(one-sided) sharp singularity
, if
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
, and there exists some
\delta>0
for which
B_{\delta}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
is a connected set;

S3:

x
is a
sharp-sharp singularity
, if
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
and for each
i\in\{1,2\}
there exists some
\delta_{i}>0
for which
U_{\delta_{i}}(x,\xi_{i})\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
is a connected set;

S4:

x
is a
(one-sided) shallow singularity
if
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
and

(i)

U_{\delta_{1}}(x,\xi_{1})\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
for some
\delta_{1}>0
, and

(ii)

U_{\delta_{2}}(x,\xi_{2})\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}\not\subset\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
for all
\delta_{2}>0
.

S5:

x
is a
shallow-shallow singularity
if
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
and
U_{\delta}(x,\xi_{i})\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}\not\subset\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
for all
\delta>0
and
i\in\{1,2\}
.

S6:

x
is a
(one-sided) chain singularity
, if
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
and
there exists a sequence of singularities
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset S(E_{\varepsilon})
,
for which
x_{n}\to x
and

\left\langle\frac{y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\rightarrow-1,

where
\big\{y_{n}^{(1)},y_{n}^{(2)}\big\}=\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})
is the set of extremal contributors at
x_{n}
;

S7:

x
is a
chain-chain singularity
, if
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
and
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
there exists some
\delta_{i}>0
and a sequence
(x_{i,n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset U_{\delta_{i}}(x,\xi_{i})\cap S(E_{\varepsilon})
, for which
x_{i,n}\to x
and

\left\langle\frac{y_{i,n}^{(1)}-x_{i,n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{i,n}^{(2)}-x_{i,n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\rightarrow-1,

where
\big\{y_{i,n}^{(1)},y_{i,n}^{(2)}\big\}=\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{i,n})
is the set of extremal contributors at
x_{i,n}
;

S8:

x
is a
sharp-chain singularity
, if
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
and

(i)

there exists a
\delta_{1}>0
for which
U_{\delta_{1}}(x,\xi_{1})\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
is a connected set, and

(ii)

there exists some
\delta_{2}>0
and a sequence
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset U_{\delta_{2}}(x,\xi_{2})\cap S(E_{\varepsilon})
, for which
x_{n}\to x
and

\left\langle\frac{y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle\rightarrow-1,

where
\big\{y_{n}^{(1)},y_{n}^{(2)}\big\}=\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})
is the set of extremal contributors at
x_{n}
.

Note that S8 may be interpreted both as a sharp singularity and as a chain singularity. Theorem
3
below states that the set of
chain singularities

\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon}):=\{x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\,:\,x\textrm{ is of type S6--S8}\}

is closed. On the other hand, the singularities of type S8 share an important property with those of types S1–S5: they all lie on the boundary
\partial V
of some connected component
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. We show in Corollary
4.3
that this is exactly the property that is lacking from singularities of type S6 and S7. See also Remark
4.4
.

Motivated by these considerations we define a boundary point to be

(i)

a
sharp singularity
, if it is of type S2, S3 or S8,

(ii)

a
chain singularity
, if it is of type S6, S7 or S8, and

(iii)

an
inaccessible singularity
, if it is of type S6 or S7.

The typology presented above is neither strictly topological nor strictly geometric. If one wanted to accomplish a strictly topological classification for neighbourhoods
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{\delta}(x)
for some
\delta:=\delta(x)>0
, types S6–S8 would necessitate an infinite tree-like classification scheme, in order to account for the potentially accumulating chain and shallow singularities in arbitrarily small neighbourhoods
B_{r}(x)
with
0<r<\delta
, see Section
5.2
and Theorem
3
.

When the complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
has positive reach, Definition
4.1
can be directly compared with
[
RZ17
, Definition 6.3]
.
1
1

1

We show in Chapter
6
that only the existence of certain kinds of chain singularities can cause the complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
not to have positive reach.
The latter defines three distinct types of geometries
T^{i}
, for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Since these apply to the complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
, they represent ’mirror images’ of the corresponding local geometry of
E_{\varepsilon}
. This classification describes the geometry at a different level of detail compared to Definition
4.1
, which can be seen as a refinement of the former. Out of the types defined in
[
RZ17
, Definition 6.3]
,
T^{1}
corresponds in our classification to smooth points, wedges (type S1) and shallow singularities (types S4 and S5),
T^{2}
corresponds to sharp-sharp (type S3), chain-chain (type S7) and sharp-chain (type S8) singularities and
T^{3}
corresponds to one-sided sharp (type S2) and chain (type S6) singularities.

4.2.
Classification of Singularities

The main goal of this section is to prove that our classification of singularities in fact defines a partition on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. We begin by characterising the topology and geometry of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
near those singularities
x\in S(E_{\varepsilon})
whose extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
satisfy
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
. Geometrically these correspond to case (
C
) in Figure
4.1
.

Proposition 4.2

(Difference between sharp-type and chain-type geometry)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
with
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
. Furthermore, let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
, let
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
be an extremal outward direction, and let
g_{\xi,y_{1}},g_{\xi,y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
be as in (
3.13
).
Then exactly one of the cases (i) and (ii) below holds true:

(i)

(sharp-type)
There exists some
r>0
, for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\neq g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)
for all
s\in(0,r)
, and

(4.1)

U_{r}(x,\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V_{\xi}\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)=\bigcup_{0<s<r}x+s\big(\alpha(s),\beta(s)\big)_{S^{1}},

where
V_{\xi}
is the unique connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
intersecting
U_{r}(x,\xi)
,
\alpha(s),\beta(s)\in S^{1}
for all
s\in(0,r)
and
\alpha(s),\beta(s)\to\xi
as
s\to 0
.

(ii)

(chain-type)
There exists a sequence
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with the following properties:

(a)

s_{n}\to 0
and
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote this common value by
x_{n}
.

(b)

There exists some
r>0
and a sequence
(V_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset U_{r}(x,\xi)
of disjoint connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
with
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(x,V_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty

and
x_{n}\in\partial V_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(c)

x_{n}\in S(E_{\varepsilon})
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with

(4.2)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\frac{y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=-1,

where
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})=\big\{y_{n}^{(1)},y_{n}^{(2)}\big\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Clearly, either there exists some
r>0
, for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\neq g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)
for all
s\in(0,r)
, or else there exists a sequence
(q_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
q_{n}\to 0
, for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(q_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(q_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and these cases are mutually exclusive. The proof amounts to showing that in the former case, representation (
4.1
) is valid for some connected component
V_{\xi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
and arc-segments
(\alpha(s),\beta(s))_{S^{1}}
, and in the latter, to identifying the prescribed sequences
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
(V_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset U_{r}(x,\xi)
, as well as confirming the limit (
4.2
) and that
x_{n}\in\partial V_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Consider for
i\in\{1,2\}
the continuous functions
f^{\xi,y_{i}}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
for which

\displaystyle g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)

\displaystyle=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)

\displaystyle=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\frac{x-y_{2}}{\varepsilon},

see Proposition
3.5
.
The assumption
x-y_{1}=-(x-y_{2})
implies that the vector representing the difference at
s\in(0,r)
between the graphs
g_{\xi,y_{1}}([0,r])
and
g_{\xi,y_{2}}([0,r])
, is given by

(4.3)

g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)-g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)=-\left(f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\right)\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}.

(i) We start by assuming that there exists some
r>0
, for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\neq g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)
for all
s\in(0,r)
which implies
\alpha_{\xi}(s):=f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\neq 0
for all
s\in(0,r)
. Due to continuity, this implies either

\textrm{(1)}\quad\alpha_{\xi}(s)>0\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,s\in(0,r),\qquad\textrm{or}\qquad\textrm{(2)}\quad\alpha_{\xi}(s)<0\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,s\in(0,r).

Note that (1) would contradict the assumption
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, so that (2) necessarily holds true. Furthermore, by the definition of the local boundary representation, and since
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
, it follows that
z\in U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\big)
if and only if
z=x+s\xi+t(x-y_{1})/\varepsilon
where
s>0
,
s^{2}+t^{2}<r^{2}
and

(4.4)

f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)<t<-f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s).

Hence, the average
h_{\xi}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, given by

(4.5)

h_{\xi}(s):=\frac{g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)}{2}=x+s\xi+\left(f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)-\frac{\alpha_{\xi}(s)}{2}\right)\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon},

satisfies
h_{\xi}(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for all
s\in(0,r)
.
Although we have defined the function
h_{\xi}
in (
4.5
) in terms of the contributor
y_{1}
, we could have equally well chosen
y_{2}
due to symmetry. Noting that
h_{\xi}
is continuous, that
h_{\xi}((0,r))\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, and
x=h_{\xi}(0)
, it follows that there exists a connected component
V_{\xi}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which
h_{\xi}((0,r))\subset V_{\xi}
and
x\in\partial V_{\xi}
. Emulating the reasoning presented in the proof of Lemma
3.9
we may confirm that
V_{\xi}
is the only connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
that intersects
U_{r}(x,\xi)
.

To obtain the representation in (
4.1
), consider for each
s\in(0,r)
the unit vectors
h(s),\alpha(s),\beta(s)\in S^{1}
, given by

(4.6)

h(s):=\frac{h_{\xi}(s)-x}{\left\lVert h_{\xi}(s)-x\right\rVert},\quad\alpha(s):=\frac{g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)-x}{\left\lVert g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)-x\right\rVert},\quad\beta(s):=\frac{g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)-x}{\left\lVert g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)-x\right\rVert}.

Proposition
2.14
and Lemma
2.13
(i) imply that the tangential directions on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
coincide with the extremal outward directions
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
at each boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. We can hence assume
r
to be small enough such that the distances

\mathcal{D}_{h}(s):=\left\lVert h_{\xi}(s)-x\right\rVert,\quad\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}(s):=\left\lVert g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)-x\right\rVert,\quad\mathcal{D}_{\beta}(s):=\left\lVert g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)-x\right\rVert

appearing in the divisors in (
4.6
) are all strictly increasing functions of
s
on the interval
(0,r)
. By definition, we furthermore have

\mathrm{max}\,\big\{\mathcal{D}_{h}^{-1}(s),\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{-1}(s),\mathcal{D}_{\beta}^{-1}(s)\big\}\leq s

for all
s\in(0,r)
. It follows that for each
s\in(0,r)

\displaystyle h_{\xi}\big(\mathcal{D}_{h}^{-1}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=x+sh\big(\mathcal{D}_{h}^{-1}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\in x+s\big(\alpha(\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}^{-1}(s)),\beta(\mathcal{D}_{\beta}^{-1}(s))\big)_{S^{1}}\subset V_{\xi}\cap U_{r}(x,\xi).

In addition, we can assume Proposition
3.1
to apply with
\delta:=\varepsilon/2
. From this it follows for
C_{s}:=s\left(S^{1}\setminus\big(\alpha(s),\beta(s)\big)_{S^{1}}\right)\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)
that

x+C_{s}\subset\overline{B_{\varepsilon}\left(E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x))\right)}\subset E_{\varepsilon}

for all
s\in(0,r)
. Hence

U_{r}(x,\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V_{\xi}\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)=\bigcup_{0<s<r}x+s\big(\alpha(s),\beta(s)\big)_{S^{1}}.

(ii) Assume then that there exists a sequence
(q_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(q_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(q_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q_{n}\to 0
. This situation corresponds to the chain-type geometry characteristic of chain singularities (types S6–S8; see Definition
4.1
and Figure
1.1
).
Note that since
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, there exists for all
s>0
some
0<\lambda<s
, for which

\alpha_{\xi}(\lambda)=f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda)<0.

One can thus define two new sequences
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
(p_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
inductively as follows. First, choose some
\lambda_{1}\in(0,q_{1})
with
f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda_{1})+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda_{1})<0
and define

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sup\left\{s\,:\,s>\lambda_{1}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda)<0\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},s]\right\},

\displaystyle p_{1}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{s\,:\,s<\lambda_{1}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda)<0\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\lambda\in[s,\lambda_{1}]\right\}.

For the induction step, assume we have already chosen the points
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n-1}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. One can then choose some
\lambda_{n}\in(0,\textrm{min}\{p_{n-1},q_{n}\})
with
f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda_{n})+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda_{n})<0
, and define

\displaystyle s_{n}

\displaystyle:=\sup\left\{s\,:\,s>\lambda_{n}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda)<0\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\lambda\in[\lambda_{n},s]\right\},

\displaystyle p_{n}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{s\,:\,s<\lambda_{n}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,f^{\xi,y_{1}}(\lambda)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(\lambda)<0\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\lambda\in[s,\lambda_{n}]\right\}.

Then
p_{n}<s_{n}\leq p_{n-1}<s_{n-1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)=0
for all
s\in(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup(p_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
. Also, by definition,
f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)<0
for all
s\in(p_{n},s_{n})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This implies that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the open set

V_{n}:=\left\{\tau g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+(1-\tau)g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\,:\,\tau\in(0,1),\,s\in(p_{n},s_{n})\right\}

is connected and satisfies
V_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
and
x_{n}:=g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{n})\in\partial V_{n}
. In addition
V_{n}\cap V_{m}=\varnothing
whenever
n\neq m
, and for every
r>0
there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, for which
0<p_{n}<s_{n}<r
for all
n\geq N
. It thus follows from Propositions
2.14
and Lemma
2.13
(i) that
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(x,V_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Since
x_{n}:=g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{n})
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist for
i\in\{1,2\}
extremal contributors
y_{n}^{(i)}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})
, for which
y_{n}^{(i)}\in B_{\varepsilon/2}(y_{i})
. This, together with Lemma
2.13
(ii)(a), implies
y_{n}^{(i)}\to y_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
as
n\to\infty
, and consequently

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\frac{y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=-1.\qed

As a consequence of Proposition
4.2
we obtain the following characterisation for inaccessible boundary points
x
, which are defined by the property that
x\notin\partial V
for all connected components
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
.

Corollary 4.3

(Inaccessible singularities)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. Then
x\notin\partial V
for all connected components
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
if and only if
x
is a one-sided chain singularity (type S6) or a chain-chain singularity (type S7).

Proof.

(i) Assume first that
x\notin\partial V
for all connected components
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Proposition
3.10
then implies that
x
is not a wedge and
x\notin\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, so that the extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
satisfy
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
.

In case
x
has only one extremal outward direction
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, Proposition
3.1
implies the existence of some
r<\varepsilon/2
for which

B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\subset U_{r}(x,\xi).

By assumption, there cannot exist a connected component
V_{\xi}
described in case (i) of Proposition
4.2
, so that case (ii) is necessarily satisfied. Hence,
x
is a (one-sided) chain singularity.

Similarly, if there exist extremal outward directions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
with
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
, Proposition
4.2
again rules out case (i) for each one of them. Consequently,
x
fulfils the definition of a chain-chain singularity.

(ii) Assume then that
x
is either a chain singularity (type S6) or a chain-chain singularity (type S7). In the former case
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}
, and Proposition
3.1
again implies the existence of some
r<\varepsilon/2
for which
B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\subset U_{r}(x,\xi)
. We aim to deduce a contradiction by assuming there exists a connected
V\subset U_{r}(x,\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which
x\in\partial V
. Note that every
z\in V
has a representation

(4.7)

z=x+s\xi+t(s)\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}

for some
s=s(z)>0
and
t(s)\in\mathbb{R}
. Now, choose some
s_{0}>0
and
t(s_{0})
so that

z_{0}:=x+s_{0}\xi+t(s_{0})\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}\in V.

Given that
V
is connected and
x\in\partial V
, it follows from
z_{0}\in V
that there exists a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to V
for which
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=z_{0}
. Following (
4.7
), we may write

\gamma(u)=x+s(u)\xi+t(s(u))\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon},

where the coordinate
s(u)
depends continuously on
u\in[0,1]
. Since
\gamma(u)\notin E_{\varepsilon}
for all
u\in[0,1]
, the coordinate
t(s(u))
satisfies

t(s(u))\in\big(f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s(u)),-f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s(u))\big)

for all
u\in[0,1]
, where the functions
f^{\xi,y_{i}}
are as in Proposition
3.5
. However, Proposition
4.2
implies the existence of some
0<q<s_{0}
for which
f^{\xi,y_{1}}(q)=-f^{\xi,y_{2}}(q)
, and since
s(\cdot)
is continuous as a function of
u
, there exists some
u_{q}
for which
s(u_{q})=q
. For this coordinate we thus obtain the contradiction

t(s(u_{q}))=t(q)\in\big(f^{\xi,y_{1}}(q),-f^{\xi,y_{2}}(q)\big)=\varnothing.

In case
x
is a chain-chain singularity (type S7), one may again follow the above reasoning to deduce that the existence of a connected component
V
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
with
x\in\partial V
would contradict the existence of a sequence
s_{n}\to 0
with
f^{\xi_{i},y_{1}}(s_{n})=-f^{\xi_{i},y_{2}}(s_{n})
, which is on the other hand guaranteed for both
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
by Proposition
4.2
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Corollary
4.3
states that it is impossible for a chain singularity (type S6) or a chain-chain singularity (type S7)
x
to lie on the boundary of any connected component
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, even though a sequence
(V_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of connected components
V_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
converges to
x
in Hausdorff distance. This is the motivation for the terminology of
inaccessible
singularities and can be seen as an analogue of the distinction between accessible and inaccessible points in a Cantor set
C\subset[0,1]
, where inaccessible points do not lie on the boundary of any of the countably many removed open intervals
[a_{n},b_{n}]\subset[0,1]
. See also Example
5.7
and the discussion after Definition
4.1
above.

Proposition 4.5

(Characterisation of chain singularities)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with the functions
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
as in (
3.13
). Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

x
is a chain singularity (type S6, S7 or S8).

(ii)

There exists a sequence
(V_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of mutually disjoint connected components
V_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}

for which
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(x,V_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

(iii)

There exists a sequence
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of singularities on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
for which
x_{n}\to x
and

(4.8)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\frac{y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=-1,

where
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})=\big\{y_{n}^{(1)},y_{n}^{(2)}\big\}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(iv)

The extremal contributors
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
satisfy
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
and there exist some
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and corresponding functions
g_{\xi,y_{1}},g_{\xi,y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
for some sequence
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
s_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Since clearly (i) implies (iii), the result then follows by showing that (iii)
\wedge
(iv)
\Rightarrow
(i).

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iv). Assume there exists a sequence
(V_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
of mutually disjoint connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
with
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(x,V_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. It follows then from Proposition
3.10
that
x\notin\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
and it cannot be a wedge, which implies
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
for the extremal contributors
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, choose a point
v_{n}\in V_{n}
, and define
\xi_{n}:=(v_{n}-x)/\left\lVert v_{n}-x\right\rVert
. Due to compactness, one can choose a subsequence
(v_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
for which
\xi_{n_{k}}\to\xi\in S^{1}
. Since
v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from Lemma
2.9
that
\langle y_{i}-x,\xi\rangle=0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, so that
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

Assume contrary to the claim that there exists
\delta>0
for which
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\neq g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)
for all
s\in(0,\delta)
. According to Proposition
4.2
this implies

(4.9)

U_{\delta}(x,\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap U_{\delta}(x,\xi)=\bigcup_{0<\rho<\delta}\rho(\alpha_{\rho},\beta_{\rho})_{S^{1}},

where
V
is the unique connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
that intersects
U_{\delta}(x,\xi)
. Since
\xi_{n_{k}}\to\xi
as
k\to\infty
, equation (
4.9
) now implies
v_{n_{k}}\in V
for large
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which in turn contradicts the assumption that the sets
V_{n}
are connected and mutually disjoint. Hence, no such
\delta
can exist, and (iv) follows.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii). Case (ii) in Proposition
4.2
now holds true, and directly implies (iii) here.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Write
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
and define
\xi_{n}:=(x_{n}-x)/\left\lVert x_{n}-x\right\rVert
. Due to Lemma
2.13
(i) there exists a subsequence
(x_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
, for which
\xi_{n_{k}}\to\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
as
k\to\infty
. Furthermore, since
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})=\big\{y_{n}^{(1)},y_{n}^{(2)}\big\}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, Lemma
2.13
(ii)(a) together with (
4.8
) implies the existence of a further subsequence
(x_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}\subset(x_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
for which
y_{m}^{(i)}\to y_{i}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. The extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}
at
x
therefore satisfy
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
.

To complete the argument, we show that for any
\delta>0
there exists some
0<s_{m}<\delta
for which
x_{m}=g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{m})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{m})
. Once this is established, the statement follows from Proposition
4.2
. Since
x_{m}\to x
as
m\to\infty
, there exists for all
\delta>0
some
M\in\mathbb{N}
for which
\left\lVert x_{m}-x\right\rVert\leq\delta
for all
m>M
. It follows that for each
m>M
there exists some
s_{m}\leq\delta
such that
x_{m}=g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s_{m})
for at least one of the contributors
y_{1},y_{2}
. But since
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
and
y_{m}^{(i)}\to y_{i}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
as
m\to\infty
, this implies
x_{m}=g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{m})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{m})
for all sufficiently large
m>M
.

(iii)
\wedge
(iv)
\Rightarrow
(i). Since
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
for the extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
, it follows from Lemma
2.10
that the extremal outward directions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
satisfy either
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
or
\xi_{1}=-\xi_{2}
. In the former case
x
is a one-sided chain singularity (type S6). In the latter case we may assume without loss of generality that
\xi_{n}:=(x_{n}-x)/\left\lVert x_{n}-x\right\rVert\to\xi_{1}
. Proposition
4.2
then states that for some
\delta>0
, the boundary subset
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap U_{\delta}(x,\xi_{2})
exhibits either sharp-type or chain-type geometry and that these cases are mutually exclusive. In the former case
x
is a sharp-chain singularity (type S8), in the latter a chain-chain singularity (type S7).
∎

We employ Proposition
4.5
to show that our characterisation of smooth points in Definition
2.2
coincides with the property of lying on a
C^{1}
-smooth curve. By a
curve
we mean the image
\Gamma=\gamma([0,1])
of a continuous, injective map
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proposition 4.6

(Characterisation of smooth points)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. Then
x
is smooth in the sense of Definition
2.2
if and only if there exists a
C^{1}
-curve
\Gamma
for which
\Gamma=\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{\delta}(x)}
for some
\delta>0
.

Proof.

(i) Assume first that
x
is smooth in the sense of Definition
2.2
. Then
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, and Proposition
2.12
implies that the set of extremal outward directions at
x
satisfies
\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi,-\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}
. In addition, let
\mathcal{G}(x)=\{g_{\xi,y},g_{-\xi,y}\}
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
. Since
x
is smooth, there exists some
0<\delta\leq r
for which
\overline{B_{\delta}(x)}\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
. In particular,
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, and it follows from Proposition
3.5
that

\Gamma:=g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{\delta}])\cup g_{-\xi,y}([0,s_{\delta}^{\prime}])=\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{\delta}(x)}

for some
s_{\delta},s_{\delta}^{\prime}\in(0,\delta]
. We will show that the functions
g_{\xi,y}
and
g_{-\xi,y}
are differentiable on the above intervals.

To this end, let
s\in[0,s_{\delta}]
and consider the point
z:=g_{\xi,y}(s)
. Then the set of extremal outward directions at
z
satisfies

(4.10)

\Xi_{z}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi_{z},-\xi_{z}\}

for some
\xi_{z}\in S^{1}
. According to Proposition
2.14
, the extremal outward directions coincide with tangential directions on the boundary. This, together with (
4.10
), implies that
g_{\xi,y}
is differentiable at
s
. Since the argument
s\in[0,s_{\delta}]
above was arbitrary, it follows that
g_{\xi_{x},y}
is differentiable on
[0,s_{\delta}]
. The differentiability of
g_{-\xi_{x},y}
on
[0,s_{\delta}^{\prime}]
, as well as the differentiability of
\Gamma
at
x
, follow with the same reasoning, and the claim follows.

(ii) Let then
\Gamma
be a
C^{1}
-curve for which
\Gamma=\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{\delta}(x)}
for some
\delta>0
. Consider now some
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{\delta}(x)}
. Since
\Gamma
is
C^{1}
-smooth, the correspondence between tangents and extremal outward directions given by Proposition
2.14
implies that
z
is not a wedge (type S1) or a sharp singularity (types S2–S3). On the other hand, since
z\in\Gamma
and
\Gamma
is connected as a curve, Proposition
4.5
implies that
z
cannot be a chain singularity (types S6–S8). Hence it follows from Theorem
1
below that
z
is either a smooth point or a shallow singularity (types S4–S5), which implies
z\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
. The same argument applies to all
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}
, which means that
x
is smooth in the sense of Definition
2.2
.
∎

4.2.1.
Proof of Theorem
1

We conclude this section with the proof of our first main result, a classification of boundary points on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. We restate the result here for the convenience of the reader.

Theorem 1

(Classification of boundary points)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact,
\varepsilon>0
and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
be a boundary point of
E_{\varepsilon}
that is not smooth. Then
x

belongs to precisely one of the eight categories of singularities given in Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

Note first that for any
u,v\in S^{1}
either
u=v
,
u=-v
, or
u\notin\{v,-v\}
. Hence we obtain the following categorisation of boundary point types according to the orientation of the extremal outward directions
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}
:

\displaystyle\xi_{1}

\displaystyle\notin\{\xi_{2},-\xi_{2}\}:

type S1

\displaystyle\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=\xi_{2}:

types S2 and S6

\displaystyle\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=-\xi_{2}:

smooth points and types S3–S5, S7–S8

These are due to Proposition
2.12
for
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
and Definition
4.1
for
x\notin\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, and they correspond to the cases (
A
)–(
C
) illustrated in Figure
4.1
. It follows immediately that if
x
is a wedge (type S1), it cannot be of any other type, and vice versa. In addition, of all the defined types of boundary points, only the shallow singularities (types S4 and S5) and smooth points satisfy
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y\}
for some
y\in\partial E
, and these types are by definition mutually exclusive.

Hence, it suffices to show that the remaining types S2–S3 and S6–S8, corresponding to case (
C
) in Figure
4.1
, are mutually exclusive. For all these types, the set of extremal contributors
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
satisfies
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
. Proposition
4.2
then states that for each
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
either

(i)

there exists a connected component
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
and
r>0
for which
U_{r}(x,\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)
and
x\in\partial V
, or

(ii)

there exists a sequence of singularities
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
S(E_{\varepsilon})
with
x_{n}\to x
as
n\to\infty
and
(x_{n}-x)/\left\lVert x_{n}-x\right\rVert\to\xi
, and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\frac{y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon},\frac{y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}}{\varepsilon}\right\rangle=-1,

and that these situations are mutually exclusive. In other words, for each extremal outward direction
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, the intersection
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)
exhibits either sharp-type or chain-type geometry (see Definition
4.1
). In case
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
, the point
x
is hence either a sharp (type S2) or a chain (type S6) singularity, and in case
\xi_{2}=-\xi_{1}
, it is either a sharp-sharp (type S3), a chain-chain (type S7), or a sharp-chain (type S8) singularity, and all these cases are mutually exclusive.
∎

Chapter 5
Topological Structure of the Set of Singularities

Since the categories of boundary points given in Definition
4.1
define a partition of the boundary, it makes sense to inquire on their cardinalities and topological structure. Our second main result, Theorem
2
, states that for any compact
E\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
, the sets of wedges (type S1), sharp singularities (types S2, S3 and S8) and one-sided chain singularities (type S6) on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
are at most countably infinite. This does not hold in general for the sets of shallow-shallow singularities (type S5) or chain-chain singularities (type S7), which may even have a positive one-dimensional Hausdorff measure on the boundary, see
[
Fer76
,
Fu85
]
. In Section
5.2
we show that the set
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
of chain singularities is nevertheless nowhere dense, and hence small in the topological sense.

5.1.
Cardinalities of Sets of Singularities

In order to prove the above-mentioned results on the cardinalities of the sets of singularities, we proceed by treating one by one the cases of wedges, sharp singularities, and one-sided shallow and chain singularities. We begin with the following general result on the geometry of accumulating singularities. It is essentially a corollary of Lemma
2.13
on the asymptotic behaviour of sequences of boundary points.

Lemma 5.1

(Geometry of accumulating singularities)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset S(E_{\varepsilon})
be a sequence of pair-wise disjoint singularities with
x_{n}\to x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. Let
\Xi_{x_{n}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\big\{\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\big|\big\langle\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\rangle\big|=1
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
2.13
(i) we can assume that the limit

\xi:=\lim_{n\to\infty}(x_{n}-x)/\left\lVert x_{n}-x\right\rVert

exists. Note that depending on the geometry at the singularities
x_{n}
, each of the extremal outward directions
\xi_{n}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
may be more aligned with
\xi
than
-\xi
, or vice versa.
However, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, Lemma
2.13
(ii)(b) implies that there exist sequences of coefficients
\big(a_{n}^{(i)}\big)_{n=1}^{\infty}\in\{1,-1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
for which
\big|\big\langle\xi_{n}^{(i)},\xi\big\rangle\big|=\big\langle a_{n}^{(i)}\xi_{n}^{(i)},\xi\big\rangle
and

(5.1)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\langle a_{n}^{(1)}\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi\big\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\langle a_{n}^{(2)}\xi_{n}^{(2)},\xi\big\rangle=1.

Let
\theta_{n}^{(i)}\geq 0
be the angle for which
\big\langle a_{n}^{(i)}\xi_{n}^{(i)},\xi\big\rangle=\cos\big(\theta_{n}^{(i)}\big)
. Equation (
5.1
) implies
\theta_{n}^{(i)}\to 0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and in particular there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
for which

\big|\big\langle\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\rangle\big|=\big\langle a_{n}^{(1)}\xi_{n}^{(1)},a_{n}^{(2)}\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\rangle

for all
n\geq N
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\theta_{n}^{\textrm{max}}:=\max\big\{\theta_{n}^{(1)},\theta_{n}^{(2)}\big\}.

Then

(5.2)

\big\langle a_{n}^{(1)}\xi_{n}^{(1)},a_{n}^{(2)}\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\rangle=\cos\big(\theta_{n}^{(1)}+\theta_{n}^{(2)}\big)\geq\cos\left(2\theta_{n}^{\textrm{max}}\right)\rightarrow 1,

as
n\to\infty
, and the result follows.
∎

A particular consequence of Lemma
5.1
is that accumulating wedges (type S1) become increasingly acute/obtuse as they approach a limit point, with the angles

\theta_{n}:=\sphericalangle\big(\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big)

between the extremal outward directions approaching an asymptotic value

\theta=\lim_{n\to\infty}\theta_{n}\in\{0,\pi\}.

It follows that for any fixed
p>0
, there can only exist finitely many wedges whose sharpness deviates from these asymptotic values by more than
p
, which in turn implies that the total number of wedges can at most be countably infinite. Lemma
5.2
below makes this argument precise.

Lemma 5.2

(Number of wedges)
.

For any compact
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
, the number of wedges (type S1) on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is at most countably infinite.

Proof.

We begin by showing that for each
p\in(0,1)
, the subset
A(p)\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
defined by

A(p):=\big\{x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\,:\,\big|\big\langle\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\big\rangle\big|\leq p\,\,\,\,\textrm{for}\,\,\,\,\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})\big\}

contains only finitely many points. Assume contrary to this that for some
p\in(0,1)
the set
A(p)
contains infinitely many points. This implies that there exists a pair-wise disjoint sequence
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
x_{n}\in A(p)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and due to compactness of
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
we may assume that
x_{n}\to x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
as
n\to\infty
. Writing
\Xi_{x_{n}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\big\{\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, Lemma
5.1
then implies
\lim_{n\to\infty}\big|\big\langle\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\rangle\big|=1
, contradicting the assumption that
\big|\big\langle\xi_{n}^{(1)},\xi_{n}^{(2)}\big\rangle\big|\leq p<1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

By definition, each wedge
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
belongs to the set
A(p)
for some
p\in(0,1)
. Hence the union

A:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}A\big(1-n^{-1}\big)

contains all the wedges on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. According to the reasoning above, each of the sets
A(1-n^{-1})
contains only finitely many points, from which the result follows.
∎

We next show that for a given connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, there can only exist finitely many sharp singularities on
\partial U
. This essentially follows from Propositions
3.5
and
4.5
which imply that any convergent sequence of pairwise disjoint sharp singularities
x_{n}\in\partial U
is associated with a sequence
(U_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
of pairwise disjoint connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, aligned with one of the extremal outward directions
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, satisfying
x_{n}\in\partial U_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Our proof by contradiction amounts to showing that there can be no fixed connected component
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which
x=\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}\in\partial U
and
U_{n}\neq U
for infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 5.3

(Number of sharp singularities)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact. For any connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, the number of sharp singularities (types S2, S3 and S8) on the boundary
\partial U
is finite.

Proof.

Assume that the claim fails for some connected
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Since
\partial U
is compact, this implies the existence of a pair-wise disjoint sequence
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\partial U
of sharp singularities with
x_{n}\to x\in\partial U
. We may furthermore assume that the sequence is ordered so that

(5.3)

\left\lVert x_{n+1}-x\right\rVert<\left\lVert x_{n}-x\right\rVert

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and that the limit
\xi:=\lim_{n\to\infty}(x_{n}-x)/\left\lVert x_{n}-x\right\rVert
exists. Write
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{n})=\big\{y_{n}^{(1)},y_{n}^{(2)}\big\}
. It follows from the definition of a sharp singularity that

(5.4)

y_{n}^{(1)}-x_{n}=-\big(y_{n}^{(2)}-x_{n}\big)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. According to Proposition
4.5
, also the extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
satisfy
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
, and
y_{n}^{(i)}\to y_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
as
n\to\infty
. Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be the local boundary representation at
x
, with radius
r>0
, given by Proposition
3.5
. Consider for
i\in\{1,2\}
the functions
g_{\xi,y_{i}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
,

g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s):=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y_{i}}(s)\frac{x-y_{i}}{\varepsilon},

where the functions
f^{\xi,y_{i}}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous. Due to (
5.4
), and since
x_{n}\to x
, there exists a sequence
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
s_{n}\to 0
and some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, for which
n>N
implies
x_{n}=g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n})=g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{n})
and consequently
f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n})+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s_{n})=0
. Inequality (
5.3
) implies
s_{n+1}<s_{n}<s_{n-1}
for all
n>N
, and one can define the open sets

\displaystyle S_{n}

\displaystyle:=\left\{s\in(s_{n+1},s_{n-1})\,:\,f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)<0\right\},

\displaystyle U_{n}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\tau g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+(1-\tau)g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\,:\,\tau\in(0,1),\,s\in S_{n}\right\}.

For each
n>N
, the set
U_{n}
is contained in the interior
\textrm{int}\,R_{n}
of the closed rectangle

\displaystyle R_{n}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{x+s\xi+t\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}\,:\,s_{n+1}\leq s\leq s_{n-1},

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\qquad\quad\inf_{s\in S_{n}}\left\{f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\right\}\leq t\leq-\inf_{s\in S_{n}}\left\{f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\right\}\Big\}.

By definition,
s_{n}\in S_{n}
for all
n>N
. From this it follows that
x_{n}\notin\partial V
for any open
V\subset R_{n}^{c}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. On the other hand,
x_{n}\in\partial U
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This implies
U\subset\textrm{int}\,R_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, since
U
is connected and
\partial R_{n}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=\varnothing
by definition. However, given that
x\in\partial U\cap R_{n}^{c}
, this leads to the contradiction
U\subset R_{n}\cap R_{n}^{c}
for all
n>N
.
∎

In
[
RZ17
, Remark 6.6]
it is noted that for any planar compact set with positive reach, the number of points of type
T^{3}
on the boundary is finite. For
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods, points of type
T^{3}
correspond to sharp singularities (type S2), see
[
RZ17
, Definition 6.3]
. Lemma
5.3
can therefore be seen as an analogue of this result in the context of
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods.

Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
below state that the sets of one-sided shallow singularities (type S4) and chain singularities (type S6) are both at most countably infinite. The argument in both cases rests on the observation that for a finite sum
M:=\sum_{x\in A}m_{x}<\infty
of non-negative real numbers
m_{x}
, indexed by some possibly uncountable set
A
, the index subset
A^{+}:=\{x\in A\,:\,m_{x}>0\}
, corresponding to the positive elements in the sum, is at most countably infinite.
1
1

1

This follows from the observation that the set
A_{n}:=\{x\in A\,:\,m_{x}>1/n\}
is finite for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and hence
A^{+}:=\{x\in A\,:\,m_{x}>0\}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_{n}
is countable as a countable union of finite sets.

In the case of shallow singularities, the numbers being summed will represent lengths (one-dimensional Hausdorff measures)
m_{x}:=\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{x})
of boundary segments
I_{x}\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. In the case of chain singularities they will stand for surface areas
m_{x}:=\mathcal{H}^{2}(A_{x})
of open subsets
A_{x}\subset\mathrm{int}\,E_{\varepsilon}
. In each case, these numbers will be strictly positive for every
x
by definition, which implies that the underlying index sets, corresponding to the sets of singularities in question, are themselves at most countably infinite.

Lemma 5.4

(Number of one-sided shallow singularities)
.

For a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the number of one-sided shallow singularities (type S4) on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is at most countably infinite.

Proof.

Let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
be a one-sided shallow singularity. Then, by definition, there exists
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and
\delta_{x}>0
for which
J_{x}(\delta_{x}):=U_{\delta_{x}}(x,\xi)\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
. In particular, it follows that if
z\neq x
is another one-sided shallow singularity, with the corresponding set
J_{z}(\delta_{z})
, then the sets
J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2)
and
J_{z}(\delta_{z}/2)
satisfy

(5.5)

J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2)\cap J_{z}(\delta_{z}/2)=\varnothing.

Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
, and let
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
be such that
J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2)=g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{x}])
for some
s_{x}\in(0,\delta)
, see Proposition
3.5
.
According to Proposition
3.6
, the function
g_{\xi,y}
is
2/\sqrt{3}
-Lipschitz, so that the length
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2))
of the curve
J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2)
satisfies

0<s_{x}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2))\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}s_{x},

see for instance
[
AFP00
, Proposition 2.49]
. Denote by
W
the set of one-sided shallow singularities. Then, due to (
5.5
), the length of the collection
I:=\bigcup_{x\in W}J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2)
satisfies

(5.6)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)=\sum_{x\in W}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{x}(\delta_{x}/2))\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\sum_{x\in W}s_{x}<\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{\varepsilon})<\infty.

The finiteness of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{\varepsilon})
was established already by Erdős, see
[
Erd45
, Section 6]
. According to the counting argument preceding the statement of the result, the estimate (
5.6
) implies that the set
W
can be at most countably infinite.
∎
