The following example demonstrates that the set of shallow-shallow singularities (type S5) can be dense and have positive Hausdorff measure on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. The idea is to construct a suitably jagged function on the interval
[0,1]
, and interpret its graph as a subset of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
of a certain set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Example 5.5

(Dense, full measure set of shallow-shallow singularities)
.

Consider a bounded, increasing function
\alpha:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
that is discontinuous at every rational number
q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
but continuous at every irrational number
p\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}
.
2
2

2

One way to construct such a function is to write
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]=\big\{q_{n}\,:\,n\in\mathbb{N}\big\}
, define
N(x):=\{n\,:\,q_{n}\leq x\}
, take any positive summable sequence
(a_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, and set
\alpha(x):=\sum_{n\in N(x)}a_{n}
for all
x\in[0,1]
. This way
\alpha
is increasing on
[0,1]
, has a jump of amplitude
a_{n}
at each rational
x=q_{n}
, is continuous at every
x\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, and satisfies
\alpha(1)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}<\infty
.

As an almost everywhere continuous bounded function, every such
\alpha
is Riemann-integrable, and its monotonicity implies that the integral function
I_{\alpha}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\alpha(s)ds
is convex. Most significantly for our example,
I_{\alpha}
has a well-defined derivative at every irrational
p\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, but not at any rational
q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, one may thus interpret the graph
G:=\big\{(s,I_{\alpha}(s))\,:\,s\in[0,1]\big\}
as a subset of an boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
as follows. Since the one-sided derivatives

D^{\pm}I_{\alpha}(s):=\lim_{h\to\pm 0}\frac{I_{\alpha}(s+h)-I_{\alpha}(s)}{h}

exist at every
s\in[0,1]
, one can define for each
x(s):=(s,I_{\alpha}(s))
the corresponding contributors
y^{-}(s),y^{+}(s)\in\Pi_{E}(x(s))
by setting

y^{\pm}(s):=\big(s+a^{\pm}(s),\,I_{\alpha}(s)-b^{\pm}(s)\big),

where for each
s\in[0,1]

a^{\pm}(s):=\frac{\varepsilon D^{\pm}I_{\alpha}(s)}{\sqrt{1+\big[D^{\pm}I_{\alpha}(s)\big]^{2}}}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad b^{\pm}(s):=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{1+\big[D^{\pm}I_{\alpha}(s)\big]^{2}}}.

A direct computation shows that
D^{\pm}I_{\alpha}(s)=b^{\pm}(s)/a^{\pm}(s)
and
\left\lVert x(s)-y^{\pm}(s)\right\rVert=\varepsilon
for all
s\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, the convexity of
I_{\alpha}
implies that for each
y^{\pm}(s)
the ball
\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(y^{\pm}(s))}
intersects
G
only at the corresponding
x(s)
, which implies that
G\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
for the set
E:=\{y^{\pm}(s)\,:\,s\in[0,1]\}
.

By construction,
y^{+}(p)=y^{-}(p)
for the irrational
p\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}
. On the other hand,
y^{+}(q)\neq y^{-}(q)
for all rational
q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
.
Hence,
x(q)
is a wedge for each rational
q
, and
x(p)\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
for every irrational
p
. This implies that the points
x(p)
are in fact shallow-shallow singularities (type S5). Due to the continuity of the integral function
I_{\alpha}
, these points form a dense set on
G
. Given that
I_{\alpha}
is convex and absolutely continuous as an integral function, and the derivative
\alpha
is bounded,
I_{\alpha}
is in fact Lipschitz continuous. It then follows from the basic properties of Hausdorff measure (see for instance
[
AFP00
, Proposition 2.49]
) and the rectifiability of
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
(see
[
RW10
, Proposition 2.3]
and
[
Fal85
, Corollary 3.3]
) that
\mathcal{H}^{1}([0,1])\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)<\infty
. Hence, the set
P:=\big\{(p,I_{\alpha}(p))\,:\,p\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}\big\}
of shallow-shallow singularities has full measure on
G
.

Lemma 5.6

(Number of one-sided chain singularities)
.

For a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the number of one-sided chain singularities (type S6) on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is at most countably infinite.

Proof.

Write
C
for the set of one-sided chain singularities on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. We argue that there exists a collection
\{A_{x}\}_{x\in C}
of pair-wise disjoint open sets
A_{x}\subset\textrm{int}\,E_{\varepsilon}
, indexed by
C
.
The result then follows from the counting argument discussed in the lead-up to Lemma
5.4
above.

For each
x\in C
, the set of outward directions is a singleton
\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi\}
. Let
x\in C
and let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
, so that for
i\in\{1,2\}
the functions
g_{\xi,y_{i}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
are given by

g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y_{i}}(s)\frac{x-y_{i}}{\varepsilon}

for some continuous functions
f^{\xi,y_{i}}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
. It follows from the definition of one-sided chain singularities that the extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
satisfy
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
for all
x\in C
. Since
-\xi\notin\Xi_{x}(E_{\varepsilon})
, Proposition
3.1
implies that there are two possibilities:

(i)

there exists some non-extremal contributor
y\in\Pi_{E}(x)
for which
\langle y-x,\xi\rangle<0
, or else

(ii)

the set

\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(E\cap U_{\delta}(y_{1},-\xi))}\cap\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(E\cap U_{\delta}(y_{2},-\xi))}\setminus\{x\}
is non-empty for all
\delta>0
.

In both cases, there exists some
p(x)<0
for which

(5.7)

Q(x):=\Big\{x+s\xi+t\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}\,:\,(s,t)\in(p(x),0)\times(-\varepsilon/2,\varepsilon/2)\Big\}\subset\mathrm{int}\,E_{\varepsilon}.

One can then define
A_{x}:=U_{p(x)/3}(x,-\xi)
. Note that for all
x\in C
, the set
A_{x}
is open and has a positive surface area
\mathcal{H}^{2}(A_{x})>0
. Furthermore,
A_{x}\subset Q(x)\subset\textrm{int}\,E_{\varepsilon}
and it follows from the construction that if
z\neq x
is any other one-sided chain singularity, its distance from
x
satisfies
|z-x|\geq p(x)
, implying
A_{x}\cap A_{z}=\varnothing
. The sets
A_{x}
are thus pair-wise disjoint, open and contained in some bounded ball
B_{R}(0)
due to the compactness of
E_{\varepsilon}
. Hence the sum
\sum_{x\in C}\mathcal{H}^{2}(A_{x})
of their surface areas is finite, from which the result follows by the counting argument discussed in the lead-up to Lemma
5.4
.
∎

5.1.1.
Proof of Theorem
2

We conclude this chapter with the proof of Theorem
2
, which combines Lemmas
5.2
–
5.4
and
5.6
into one statement.

Theorem 2

(Countable sets of singularities)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then
the corresponding
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
contains at most a countably infinite number of wedges (type S1), sharp singularities (types S2, S3, S8), one-sided shallow singularities (type S4) and chain singularities (type S6).

Proof.

Consider the collection
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of the connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Since
E
is assumed to be compact,
E\subset B_{R}(0)
for some
R>0
. It follows that all but one, say
U_{j}
, of the connected components
U_{i}
are bounded, so that

\bigcup_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}U_{i}\subset B_{R}(0).

Following the counting argument discussed before the statement of Lemma
5.4
, this implies that the index set
I
is at most countably infinite. By definition, every sharp singularity
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
(types S2, S3, S8) satisfies
x\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\partial U_{i}
. It follows then from Lemma
5.3
that the set of sharp singularities on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is countable as a countable union of finite sets. Finally, Lemmas
5.2
,
5.4
and
5.6
guarantee that the number of wedges (type S1) and one-sided shallow (type S4) and chain (type S6) singularities, respectively, are at most countably infinite.
∎

5.2.
Chain Singularities Form a Totally Disconnected Set

We conclude our analysis of the singularities on the boundary by showing that the set
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
of chain singularities (types S6–S8) is closed and totally disconnected. This implies that
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
is nowhere dense, meaning that it is small in the topological sense, even though it may have a positive one-dimensional Hausdorff measure on the boundary.

Before presenting the proof of the above result, we provide a concrete example of a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
for which the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the set of chain-chain singularities on
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is positive. Essentially, we analyse
[
RW10
, Example 2.2]
from the geometric point of view.

Example 5.7

(A set of chain singularities with positive measure)
.

Let
C\subset[0,1]
be a ’fat’ Cantor set (a Cantor set with positive one-dimensional Hausdorff measure) and consider the set
E:=\big\{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,s\in C,\,t\in\{0,1\}\big\}
. The Cantor set is obtained by removing from the interval
[0,1]
a certain countable collection
\mathcal{I}:=\{I_{n}\,:\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
of open subintervals. By construction,
C
is totally disconnected and thus contains no intervals. Since it is uncountable, most of the points
s\in C
do not lie on the boundary of any of the removed intervals. Denote the collection of these points by
C^{*}:=C\setminus\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\partial I_{n}
. By construction, every
s\in C^{*}
is however an accumulation point of
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}I_{n}
. Since the sets
I_{n}
are open, it follows that for
\varepsilon=1/2
the sets
V_{n}:=\{(s,1/2)\,:\,s\in I_{n}\}
satisfy
V_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for every point
x\in A:=\{(s,1/2)\,:\,s\in C^{*}\}\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
there exists a sequence
(w_{m})_{m=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, where
w_{m}=(s_{m},1/2)\in V_{m}
for some
s_{m}\in I_{n(m)}
, and
s_{m}\to s
as
m\to\infty
. We can also assume that
I_{n(m)}\neq I_{n(m^{\prime})}
whenever
m\neq m^{\prime}
. For the connected components
W_{m}\supset V_{m}
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, this implies
W_{m}\neq W_{m^{\prime}}
whenever
m\neq m^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that condition (ii) in Proposition
4.5
thus holds true for all
x\in A
so that
A\subset\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
. Hence,
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon}))\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(A)=\mathcal{H}^{1}(C^{*})>0
due to the translation invariance of Hausdorff measure.

5.2.1.
Proof of Theorem
3

The proof of our third main result builds on many of the results presented in the previous sections. To show that the set of chain singularities is closed, we combine Proposition
3.10
regarding the connectedness of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
near wedges and
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
with the characterisation of chain singularities provided by Proposition
4.5
. The second part of the proof also makes use of the basic results established in Section
2.2
.

Theorem 3

(The set of chain singularities is closed and totally disconnected)
.

For any compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
, the set
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
of chain singularities is closed and totally disconnected.

Proof.

We begin by showing that the complement
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
is open. To this end, consider some
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
. If
x
is a wedge (type S1) or if
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
, Proposition
3.10
implies that there exists some neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
and a connected subset
V_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for which

(5.8)

B_{r}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=B_{r}(x)\cap V_{x}.

One the other hand, Proposition
4.5
states that each chain singularity
x\in\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
is associated with a sequence
(V_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
of disjoint connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, for which
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(x,V_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Equation (
5.8
) hence implies that
B_{r}(x)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\varnothing
. Similarly, for a sharp singularity (type S2) or a sharp-sharp singularity (type S3), Proposition
4.2
implies the existence of a neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
for which
B_{r}(x)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\varnothing
. Hence
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
is open on the boundary.

To demonstrate that
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
is totally disconnected, we show that for any two chain singularities
x,z\in\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
there exist disjoint open sets
A_{x},A_{z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
for which
x\in A_{x},z\in A_{z}
and
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})\subset(A_{x}\cup A_{z})\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. More specifically, we will consider for each
x\in\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
and
s_{1}\leq 0\leq s_{2}
the sets

(5.9)

A_{x,\xi}(s_{1},s_{2}):=\Big\{x+s\xi+t\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}\,:\,s_{1}<s<s_{2},\,\,-\varepsilon/2<t<\varepsilon/2\Big\}

and show that for each
z\in\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})\setminus\{x\}
there exist
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and
s_{1}<0<s_{2}
for which
\partial A_{x,\xi}(s_{1},s_{2})\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\varnothing
and
z\notin A_{x,\xi}(s_{1},s_{2})
.

Given that
x
is a chain singularity, Proposition
4.2
implies that for
r>0
the boundary region
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)
exhibits chain-type geometry near
x
for at least one extremal outward direction
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. We begin by assuming that
\xi
is such a direction, and consider the corresponding sets
A_{x,\xi}(0,s)
for
s>0
. Writing
R(z):=\left\lVert z-x\right\rVert
, our aim is to find some
s<R(z)/2
for which
\partial A_{x,\xi}(0,s)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\{x\}
.
To this end, let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
and let
f^{\xi,y_{1}},f^{\xi,y_{2}}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
be the continuous functions for which

g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y_{i}}(s)\frac{x-y_{i}}{\varepsilon}

for every
g_{\xi,y_{i}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
,
i\in\{1,2\}
and all
s\in[0,r]
. As argued in the proof of Proposition
4.2
(ii), there exist sequences
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
(p_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
for which

•

s_{n}\to 0
and
p_{n}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
,

•

p_{n}<s_{n}\leq p_{n-1}<s_{n-1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)=0
for all
s\in(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup(p_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, and

•

f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)<0
for all
s\in(p_{n},s_{n})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

It follows that the open set

V_{n}:=\big\{\tau g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+(1-\tau)g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\,:\,\tau\in(0,1),\,s\in(p_{n},s_{n})\big\}

is a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
for which
\mathrm{dist}_{H}(V_{n},x)<R(z)/2
whenever
n\geq N
. The definition of the sets
V_{n}
implies that for each
n\geq N
, the boundary point
x_{n}^{(1)}:=g_{\xi,y_{1}}\big(\frac{p_{n}+s_{n}}{2}\big)\in\partial V_{n}\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
has an outward direction aligned with the vector

\displaystyle\eta_{n}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=g_{\xi,y_{2}}\left(\frac{p_{n}+s_{n}}{2}\right)-g_{\xi,y_{1}}\left(\frac{p_{n}+s_{n}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(f^{\xi,y_{1}}\left(\frac{p_{n}+s_{n}}{2}\right)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}\left(\frac{p_{n}+s_{n}}{2}\right)\right)(x-y_{1})

An expression analogous to the above, obtained by replacing the roles of
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, holds true for
x_{n}^{(2)}
and
\eta_{n}^{(2)}
, defined similarly.
We claim that there exists some
n\geq N
, for which
x_{n}^{(i)}\notin\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Assume this were not the case. Then it follows from the definition of chain-singularities and Proposition
2.12
that for at least one
i\in\{1,2\}
we have
\eta_{n}^{(i)}/\big\|\eta_{n}^{(i)}\big\|\in\Xi_{x_{n}^{(i)}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
for infinitely many
n\geq N
. By virtue of the definition of the sets
V_{n}
as regions between the graphs
g_{\xi,y_{i}}
, and since
x_{n}^{(i)}\in\partial V_{n}\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from Proposition
2.14
that

\xi=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{x_{n}^{(i)}-x}{\big\|x_{n}^{(i)}-x\big\|}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
. But then, due to Lemma
2.13
(ii)(b), we have

0=\left\langle\frac{x-y_{i}}{\varepsilon},\,\xi\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\frac{\eta_{n}^{(i)}}{\big\|\eta_{n}^{(i)}\big\|},\xi\right\rangle=1,

which is impossible. Thus, there exists some
n\in\mathbb{N}
for which
x_{n}^{(i)}\notin\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, which in turn implies that
\partial A_{x,\xi}\big(0,(p_{n}+s_{n})/2\big)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\{x\}
, since

\partial A_{x,\xi}\left(0,\frac{p_{n}+s_{n}}{2}\right)\cap\partial E_{\varepsilon}=\big\{x,x_{n}^{(1)},x_{n}^{(2)}\big\}.

To conclude the proof we consider one by one the cases of one-sided chain (type S6), chain-chain (type S7) and sharp-chain (type S8) singularities, and identify the sets
A_{x}
and
A_{z}
mentioned in the beginning of the proof.

(i) Assume
x
is a one-sided chain singularity (type S6), so that
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}
. By the argument presented above, there exists some
0<s_{2}<R(z)/2
for which
\partial A_{x,\xi}\big(0,s_{2}\big)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\{x\}
. On the other hand, according to the reasoning presented in the proof of Lemma
5.6
, the set

(5.10)

Q_{p}(x):=\Big\{x+s\xi+t\frac{x-y_{1}}{\varepsilon}\,:\,p<s<0,\,-\varepsilon/2<t<\varepsilon/2\Big\}

satisfies
Q_{p}(x)\subset\mathrm{int}\,E_{\varepsilon}
for some
p<0
(see equation (
5.7
)). By setting
s_{1}:=-\mathrm{min}\big\{p,R(z)/2\big\}
it follows then that
\partial A_{x,\xi}(s_{1},s_{2})\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\varnothing
and we may define
A_{x}:=A_{x,\xi}(s_{1},s_{2})
and
A_{z}:=\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{A_{x}}\big)
.

(ii) Assume then that
x
is a chain-chain singularity (type S7) with
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi,-\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}
. Since now both of the extremal outward directions
\xi
and
-\xi
are associated with chain-type geometry, one can again utilise the argument above in order to choose for
\xi_{1}:=\xi
and
\xi_{2}:=-\xi
the corresponding
s_{1},s_{2}>0
for which
\partial A_{x,\xi_{i}}\big(0,s_{i}\big)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\{x\}
and
s_{i}<R(z)/2
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. By setting
s_{3}=\mathrm{min}\{s_{1},s_{2}\}
we may define
A_{x}:=A_{x,\xi}(-s_{3},s_{3})
and
A_{z}:=\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{A_{x}}\big)
.

(iii) Finally, assume that
x
is a sharp-chain singularity (type S8) with
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi,-\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}
. We may assume that
\xi
is associated with chain-type geometry, so that once again we have
\partial A_{x,\xi}\big(0,s_{2}\big)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\{x\}
for some
0<s_{2}<R(z)/2
due to the arguments presented above. For the direction
-\xi
, Proposition
4.2
(i) implies that there exists some
r>0
for which

U_{r}(x,-\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=V\cap U_{r}(x,-\xi)=\bigcup_{0<s<r}x+s\big(\alpha(s),\beta(s)\big)_{S^{1}},

where
V
is the unique connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
intersecting
U_{r}(x,-\xi)
,
\alpha(s),\beta(s)\in S^{1}
for all
s\in(0,r)
and
\alpha(s),\beta(s)\to-\xi
as
s\to 0
. It follows then from the definition of chain singularity that
U_{r}(x,-\xi)\cap\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=\varnothing
. Finally, by setting
s_{1}=-\mathrm{min}\{r/2,\varepsilon/2,R(z)/2\}
we may thus define
A_{x}:=A_{x,\xi}(s_{1},s_{2})
and
A_{z}:=\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{A_{x}}\big)
, which completes the proof.
∎

The set
\mathcal{I}
of inaccessible singularities (types S6 and S7) inherits the properties of being totally disconnected and nowhere dense, but it may generally fail to be closed. Since the set
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
on the other hand is compact and separable as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, it follows from the Cantor-Bendixson Theorem (see
[
Kec95
, Thm. 6.4]
) that whenever the cardinality of the chain-chain singularities (type S7) is uncountable, the set
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
can be written as a disjoint union
\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})=C\cup P
, where
C
is homeomorphic to the Cantor set and
P
is countable. For further information on totally disconnected spaces, see
[
Kec95
]
.

Chapter 6
Complements of -neighbourhoods as Sets with Positive Reach

In this chapter we characterise, in terms of local topology around boundary singularities, those
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods
E_{\varepsilon}
whose complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is a set with positive reach, see Definition
1.2
. To reach this, we make use of
[
Fu85
, Corollary 3.4]
, which implies that the complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}=d_{E}^{-1}([\varepsilon,\infty))
has positive reach at the regular values
\varepsilon
of the distance function

d_{E}(x):=\inf_{y\in E}\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert.

We combine this with Ferry’s geometric characterisation (
1.6
) of the critical points of
d_{E}
and then interpret the situation in terms of our classification of boundary points, given in Theorem
1
.

It follows from Theorem
1
that condition (
1.6
) is satisfied at a boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
if and only if there exist extremal contributors
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
for which

(6.1)

y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x).

This corresponds to the local geometry type (
C
) in Figure
4.1
, and means that
x
is either a sharp singularity (types S2 and S3) or a chain singularity (types S6–S8). It follows from this that if
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
has diameter less than
2\varepsilon
, then the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
contains no critical points of the distance function
d_{E}
. The set
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
then has positive reach according to
[
Fu85
, Corollary 3.4]
. We use this observation, in conjunction with the local boundary representations given by Proposition
3.5
, to show that for any compact
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the local reach (
1.8
) of the set
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is positive everywhere, with the possible exception of sharp and chain singularities, where the local reach may be zero.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact,
\varepsilon>0
and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
be either a smooth point, a wedge (type S1) or a shallow singularity (types S4 and S5). Then
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)>0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
. We first argue that for each extremal contributor
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
, there exists some
\delta(y)>0
for which the set
A(y):=E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(y)
satisfies

(6.2)

\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y))},x\big)=\delta(y).

Since
\mathrm{diam}\big(A(y)\big)<2\varepsilon
, equation (
6.1
) cannot be satisfied for any boundary point
x\in\partial B_{\varepsilon}(A_{y})).
Condition (
1.6
) thus guarantees that the boundary
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(A(y))
contains no critical points of the distance function
d_{A(y)}
. Thus,
[
Fu85
, Corollary 3.4]
implies that the set
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y))}
has positive reach, which in particular implies (
6.2
) for some
\delta(y)>0
.

If
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
(i.e. if
x
is a smooth point or a shallow singularity), there is a unique extremal contributor
y
so that
\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)=\{y\}
. It then follows from Proposition
3.1
that in the neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
corresponding to the local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
we have

B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}=B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y))}.

Due to (
6.2
) we know that
B_{\delta(y)}(x)\subset\mathrm{Unp}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y))}\big)
, see Definition
1.2
. Reducing the radius of this neighbourhood around
x
further to
\rho(x):=\textrm{min}\{r,\delta(y)\}/3
guarantees that for all
z\in B_{\rho(x)}(x)
we in fact have

\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}}(z)=\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y))}}(z),

where
\Pi
is the metric projection (
1.7
). Hence,
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)\geq\rho(x)
whenever
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
.

Assume then that
x
is a wedge singularity. The set of extremal pairs at
x
is then of the form
\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{(\xi_{1},y_{1}),(\xi_{2},y_{2})\}
, with
\xi_{1}\neq\pm\xi_{2}
. Proposition
3.1
implies that

B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}=B_{r}(x)\cap\bigcap_{i=1,2}\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))},

where the radius
r>0
corresponds to the local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
. Moreover, it follows from Proposition
3.1
that the set
B_{r}(x)\cap E_{\varepsilon}
can be decomposed into the disjoint union

\displaystyle B_{r}(x)\cap E_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\Big(U_{r}(x,\xi_{1})\cap B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{1}))\Big)\cup\Big(U_{r}(x,\xi_{2})\cap B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{2}))\Big)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\qquad\qquad\cup\Big(B_{r}(x)\setminus\big(U_{r}(x,\xi_{1})\cup U_{r}(x,\xi_{2})\big)\Big).

Let
\rho(x):=\textrm{min}\{r,\delta(y_{1}),\delta(y_{2})\}/3
, where
\delta(y_{i})
is given by (
6.2
) for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Similarly to the case
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
described above, restricting to the smaller neighbourhood
B_{\rho(x)}(x)
allows us to infer that

\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}}(z)=\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))}}(z)

for
i\in\{1,2\}
and all
z\in U_{\rho(x)}(x,\xi_{i})\cap B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))
. For these
z
, we thus have
z\in\mathrm{Unp}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}\big)
, due to (
6.2
). In case
z\in B_{\rho(x)}(x)\setminus\big(U_{\rho(x)}(x,\xi_{1})\cup U_{\rho(x)}(x,\xi_{2})\big)
we have
\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}}(z)=\{x\}
, which again implies
z\in\mathrm{Unp}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}\big)
. It thus follows that
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)\geq\rho(x)>0
.
∎

We now proceed to characterise those boundary points
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
which satisfy
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)=0
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact,
\varepsilon>0
and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. Then
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)=0
if and only if the set
B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is disconnected for all
r>0
.

Proof.

According to Theorem
1
, each boundary point belongs to exactly one of the following categories in terms of its local geometry (see Figure
4.1
for an illustration):

(a)

x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
(smooth points and shallow singularities),

(b)

\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\}
with
\xi_{1}\notin\{\xi_{2},-\xi_{2}\}
(wedges), or

(c)

there exist
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
for which
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
(sharp singularities and chain singularities).

For
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
in categories (a) and (b), Lemma
6.1
implies
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)>0
, and Proposition
3.10
guarantees the existence of some
r>0
for which
B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is connected.
Thus, we need to verify the claim for boundary points in category (c). Note that in the case of chain singularities, the set
B_{r}(x)\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\big)
is by definition disconnected for all
r>0
, but that this is not necessarily true for the set
B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
. The proof below hinges on this fact, which intuitively expresses the observation that there may or may not be a non-zero distance between the disjoint connected components
V_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, defined in Proposition
4.5
, which constitute the complement of the set
E_{\varepsilon}
near the point
x
.

Let
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. Then, if
x
belongs to category (c) above, the half-ball
U_{r}(x,\xi)
exhibits either sharp-type or chain-type geometry as defined in Proposition
4.2
(i)-(ii). The same holds true for
U_{r}(x,-\xi)
, unless
x
is a one-sided sharp singularity or a one-sided chain singularity. In this latter case,
U_{r}(x,-\xi)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. This implies
\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}}(z)=\{x\}
for
z\in U_{r/3}(x,-\xi)
, so that
U_{r/3}(x,-\xi)\subset\mathrm{Unp}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon})
. To prove the claim, it thus suffices to analyse the geometry of
E_{\varepsilon}
inside the half-balls
U_{r}(x,\xi)
for
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

Assume now that for some
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, the set
U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is disconnected for all
r>0
. The same then holds true also for the set
U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\big)
, which consequently exhibits chain-type geometry as defined in Proposition
4.2
(ii).
Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
with radius
r>0
, let
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and let
g_{\xi,y_{1}},g_{\xi,y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
, see Proposition
3.5
.
For
i\in\{1,2\}
, consider the continuous functions
f^{\xi,y_{i}}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
for which

(6.3)

g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y_{i}}(s)\frac{x-y_{i}}{\varepsilon}.

Since
y_{1}-x=-(y_{2}-x)
, it follows from the definition of the local boundary representation that
z\in U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\big)
if and only if
z=x+s\xi+t(x-y_{1})/\varepsilon
,
where
s>0
,
s^{2}+t^{2}<r^{2}
and

(6.4)

f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)<t<-f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s).

As argued in the proof of Proposition
4.2
(ii), the above characterisation in terms of (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), together with the disconnectedness of
U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}\big)
for all
r>0
, implies that there exist sequences
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
(p_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
for which

•

s_{n}\to 0
and
p_{n}\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
,

•

p_{n}<s_{n}\leq p_{n-1}<s_{n-1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)=0
for all
s\in(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup(p_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, and

•

f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)<0
for all
s\in(p_{n},s_{n})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

It follows then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
that
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n}),g_{\xi,y_{1}}(p_{n})\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and the open set

(6.5)

V_{n}:=\big\{\tau g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+(1-\tau)g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)\,:\,\tau\in(0,1),\,s\in(p_{n},s_{n})\big\}

is a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Crucially, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the union
\overline{V_{n}}\cup\overline{V_{n+1}}
is a connected set if and only if
\overline{V_{n}}\cap\overline{V_{n+1}}\neq\varnothing
, which is equivalent to
s_{n+1}=p_{n}
. Geometrically, this means that the closures of the consecutive sets
V_{n}
and
V_{n+1}
touch at the point
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n+1})=g_{\xi,y_{1}}(p_{n})
.

The above reasoning implies that the union
\bigcup_{n\geq M}\overline{V_{n}}
is disconnected for all
M\in\mathbb{N}
. This in turn implies that
0
is an accumulation point of the set

(6.6)

S:=\big\{s\in(0,r)\,:\,f^{\xi,y_{1}}(s)+f^{\xi,y_{2}}(s)>0\big\}.

Consequently, there exist subsequences
(s_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
and
(p_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
for which the pairs
(s_{n_{k}},p_{n_{k}-1})
satisfy
s_{n_{k}}<p_{n_{k}-1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Geometrically, the proper inequality here indicates a gap of positive length between the consecutive connected components
V_{n_{k}}
and
V_{n_{k}-1}
. Now, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the midpoint

x_{k}:=\frac{g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n_{k}})+g_{\xi,y_{1}}(p_{n_{k}-1})}{2}\in\mathrm{int}\,E_{\varepsilon}.

Let
\rho:=\left\lVert g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n_{k}})-g_{\xi,y_{1}}(p_{n_{k}-1})\right\rVert/2
. Then

B_{\rho}(x_{k})\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}=\{g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n_{k}}),g_{\xi,y_{1}}(p_{n_{k}-1})\}.

It follows that

\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}}(x_{k})=\{g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s_{n_{k}}),g_{\xi,y_{1}}(p_{n_{k}-1})\},

so that
x_{k}\notin\mathrm{Unp}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}\big)
.
Since
x_{k}\to x
as
k\to\infty
, this implies
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)=0
as claimed.

To prove the implication in the other direction, assume there exists some
r>0
for which the sets
U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
are connected for each
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. According to Proposition
4.2
, these sets exhibit either sharp-type or chain-type geometry. For sharp-type geometry, the set
U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
consists of a single connected set, which implies that the graphs
g_{\xi,y_{i}}((0,r])
,
i\in\{1,2\}
do not touch. In the case of chain-type geometry, these sets are composed of a (countable) collection of sets of type (
6.5
) which thus must form an unbroken chain. Hence, the set of indices (
6.6
) contains only isolated points. Geometrically this means that the graphs
g_{\xi,y_{i}}([0,r])
,
i\in\{1,2\}
do not cross transversally.

Analogously to the proof of Lemma
6.1
, Proposition
3.1
implies for each
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
the decomposition

U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap E_{\varepsilon}=\Big(U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{1}))\Big)\cup\Big(U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{2}))\Big).

Now, as argued in the proof of Lemma

6.1
, it follows from
[
Fu85
, Corollary 3.4]
that for
i\in\{1,2\}
the set
A(y_{i}):=E\cap B_{\varepsilon/2}(y_{i})
satisfies

(6.7)

\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))},x\big)>0.

Since the graphs
g_{\xi,y_{i}}([0,r])
do not cross, we furthermore have

U_{r}(x,\xi)\cap\partial B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))=\{g_{\xi,y_{i}}(s)\,:\,s\in[0,r]\}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Hence,

\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}}(z)=\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))}}(z)

whenever
z\in U_{r/3}(x,\xi)\cap B_{\varepsilon}(A(y_{i}))
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. It then follows from (
6.7
) that these projections are singletons, so that
\textrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}\big)>0
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
6.2
shows that for all sharp singularities (types S2 and S3) and even for certain chain singularities (types S6–S8), the local
\textrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)
is strictly positive. Combining this with Lemma
6.1
we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.3

(Non-chain singularities have positive reach)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact,
\varepsilon>0
and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. If
x
is not a chain singularity (types S6–S8), then
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)>0
, and there exists some
r>0
for which the set
B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is connected.

Around sharp singularities, and those chain singularities that have positive local reach, the geometry of the set
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
corresponds to the geometric types
T^{2}
(for sharp-sharp (type S3) and chain-chain (type S7) singularities) and
T^{3}
(for sharp (type S2) and chain (type S6) singularities) presented in
[
RZ17
, Definition 6.3]
. In particular, it follows that the existence of critical points of the distance function
d_{E}
on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
does not directly imply that the set
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
fails to have positive reach.

6.0.1.
Proof of Theorem
4

We now provide the proof of our fourth main result, Theorem
4
, which characterises, in terms of a local connectedness property, those
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods whose complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is a set with positive reach.

Theorem 4

(Local geometry of
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods whose complements have positive reach)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and
\varepsilon>0
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

the complement
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is a set with positive reach,

(ii)

for each
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, there exists some
r:=r(x)>0
for which the set
B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is connected.

(iii)

for each chain singularity
x\in\mathcal{C}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
, there exists some
r:=r(x)>0
for which the set
B_{r}(x)\cap\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
is connected.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Assume the set
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}}
has positive reach. Then, by definition,

\inf_{x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)>0.

In particular, there exist no points
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
for which
\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x\big)=0
. Then, (ii) follows from Proposition
6.2
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Trivial.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Follows directly from Corollary
6.3
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Assume that (i) fails. This implies that there exists a sequence
(x_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
with

(6.8)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x_{n}\big)=0.

Since
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is compact, there exists a convergent subsequence
(x_{n_{k}})_{k=1}^{\infty}
with
x_{0}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{n_{k}}\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
.
Now, combining (ii) with Proposition
6.2
implies that there exists some
r:=r(x_{0})>0
for which

(6.9)

\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x_{0}\big)>r.

According to (
6.8
) there exists some
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k>k_{0}
we have simultaneously
\left\lVert x_{n_{k}}-x_{0}\right\rVert<r/2
and

(6.10)

\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x_{n_{k}}\big)<r/4.

On the other hand, for a fixed
K>k_{0}
, it follows from
\left\lVert x_{n_{K}}-x\right\rVert<r/2
and (
6.9
) that

\mathrm{reach}\big(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}},x_{k_{N}}\big)\geq r/2,

since
B_{r/2}(x_{k_{N}})\subset B_{r}(x_{0})
. This contradicts (
6.10
) and finishes the proof.
∎

Chapter 7
Jordan Curves on the Boundary

In this chapter we show that the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is the disjoint union of a possibly uncountable set
\mathcal{I}
of inaccessible singularities and an at most countably infinite collection
J
of Jordan curves. By definition, inaccessible singularities do not lie on the boundary of any connected component of the complement. These correspond to
chain
(type S6) and
chain-chain singularities
(type S7), see Figure
1.2
(
B
). To prove Theorem
5
, it thus suffices to obtain a representation for the boundaries of connected components of the complement as a union of Jordan curves.
Intuitively, these Jordan curves can be constructed by connecting adjacent simple curves that represent local boundary segments. There is an essentially unique way of connecting such curve representations around all types of boundary points, except sharp-sharp singularities, where the boundary curves ’split’, see Figure
7.1
. However, it turns out that for each connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, there exists a unique way of connecting the curves around sharp-sharp singularities that results in a representation of the boundary
\partial U
as a finite union of Jordan curves.

7.1.
The Role of Sharp-sharp Singularities in Boundary Topology

Let
U
be a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
and let
Q
denote the set of sharp-sharp singularities on
\partial U
. According to Lemma
5.3
, the set
Q
is finite, and Proposition
4.2
implies that each
x\in Q
lies on the boundary of at most two connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
.
Hence, the set
Q
can be written as the disjoint union

(7.1)

Q=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2},

where
Q_{1}
contains those sharp-sharp singularities that lie on the boundary of a unique connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, and
Q_{2}
contains the sharp-sharp singularities for which there exist two disjoint connected components
U,V\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
with
x\in\partial U\cap\partial V
. The fact that both
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
may be non-empty is the reason sharp-sharp singularities play a central role for the topological structure of the boundary
\partial U
.

Figure 7.1
.
Topology of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
around sharp-sharp singularities. The figures here are close-ups of the points
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
in Figure
1.2
(
A
). (
A
) The sharp-sharp singularity
x_{1}\in Q_{1}
lies on the boundary of a single connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Here the curves
\Gamma_{x_{1}}^{1}
(blue) and
\Gamma_{x_{1}}^{2}
(red) pass through
x_{1}
along the boundary subsets
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
. (
B
) The point
x_{3}\in Q_{2}
lies on the boundary of two different connected components
U,V\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. The curve
\Gamma_{x_{3}}
(red) bounces back at
x_{3}
along the boundary subset
J_{2}
.

The boundary
\partial U
of a single connected component
U
may thus consist of more than one Jordan curve for two reasons. First,
U
need not be simply connected, and can thus surround several connected components
A\subset E_{\varepsilon}
. The boundary of each such
A
contains at least one Jordan curve. Second, if
\partial U
contains sharp-sharp singularities of type
Q_{1}
, more than one Jordan curve is needed to represent the boundary. Any single curve containing the boundary subset
B_{r}(x)\cap\partial U
for any
x\in Q_{1}
would intersect itself at
x
, and would thus not be a Jordan curve.

7.2.
Boundaries of Connected Components of the Complement

Let the sets
U
and
Q=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
be as above. According to Proposition
3.5
, each
x\in\partial U
has a neighbourhood
B_{x}:=B_{r}(x)
,
within which the local boundary subset
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}
can be represented as a union of either two or four graphs of continuous functions. We show in Lemma
7.2
below that it is possible to use these functions to represent the boundary subset
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}
with either one (for
x\in\partial U\setminus Q_{1}
) or two (for
x\in Q_{1}
) simple curves.

Before stating the lemma, we define the basic terminology regarding curves. We give the definition in a general setting, but we will only be dealing with plane curves, i.e. curves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Definition 7.1

(Curve, simple curve, Jordan curve)
.

A
curve
is the image
\Gamma=\gamma([a,b])
of a closed interval
[a,b]
under some continuous map
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\gamma
is injective, we call
\Gamma
a
simple curve
, and if injectivity is violated only at the endpoints, i.e.
\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
, then we say that
\Gamma
is a
simple closed curve
or a
Jordan curve
.

Lemma 7.2

(Boundaries of connected components of the complement as unions of simple curves)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact, let
\varepsilon>0
and let
U
be a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. For each
x\in\partial U
, let
B_{x}:=B_{r}(x)
be the neighbourhood in (
3.14
), corresponding to the local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
given by Proposition
3.5
. In addition, denote by
Q
the set of sharp-sharp singularities on
\partial U
, and let the subsets
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subset Q
be as in (
7.1
).
Then

(i)

for each
x\in Q_{1}
there exist continuous functions
\gamma_{x}^{(1)},\gamma_{x}^{(2)}:[0,1]\to\partial U
for which the images
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}:=\gamma_{x}^{(i)}([0,1])
with
i\in\{1,2\}
are simple curves,
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cup\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cap\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}=\{x\}
;

(ii)

for each
x\in\partial U\setminus Q_{1}
there exists a continuous function
\gamma_{x}:[0,1]\to\partial U
, for which the image
\Gamma_{x}:=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
is a simple curve and
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}
.

Proof.

Figure 7.2
.
Local curve representations. In each picture,
x
represents the boundary point in question, the grey region represents the set
E_{\varepsilon}
, and white regions the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. The red dashed balls correspond to the neighbourhoods
B_{r}(x)
associated with the local boundary representations
\mathcal{G}(x)
, see (
3.14
). The dark red curves (and the dark blue curve for
x\in Q_{1}
) represent the simple curve representations
\Gamma_{x}=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
of
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}
. Chain (type S6) and chain-chain (type S7) singularities are absent as these do not appear on the boundaries of connected components of the complement, see Corollary
4.3
. The types
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
together represent sharp-sharp (type S3) singularities. Note that points of type
Q_{1}
are the only ones for which two simple curves are needed in a local representation of the boundary of a fixed connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, see also Figure
7.1
.

We divide the proof into two steps.
In the first step, we define the local curve representations of the boundary
\partial U
around sharp-sharp singularities
x\in Q
. We also show that the images
\Gamma_{x}
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}
are simple (i.e. non-self-intersecting) curves, and that
\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cap\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}\cap B_{r}(x)=\{x\}
for sufficiently small radii
r>0
.
In the second step, we define curve representations, analogous to those defined in Step 1, for the other types of boundary points
x\in\partial U\setminus Q
.

Step 1.
Consider a boundary point
x\in\partial U
, and let
\mathcal{G}(x)=\{g_{\xi,y}\,:\,(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})\}
be a local boundary representation at
x
, with the functions
g_{\xi,y}
defined as in (
3.13
).
By the definition of sharp-sharp singularities, the set of extremal outward directions for each
x\in Q
satisfies
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi,-\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, see Definition
4.1
. Proposition
4.2
furthermore states that there exist connected components
V_{\xi},V_{-\xi}
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, for which

(7.2)

\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}\subset\big(\partial V_{\xi}\cup\partial V_{-\xi}\big)\cap\overline{B_{x}}.

Since
Q\subset\partial U
, we have
U\in\{V_{\xi},V_{-\xi}\}
and we may define
\xi
so that
U=V_{\xi}
. The correct way of defining the local curve representation near
x
now depends on whether
x\in Q_{1}
or
x\in Q_{2}
, where
Q=Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}
as in (
7.1
). We deal with these cases separately as follows:

(i)

Let
x\in Q_{1}
. This implies that
U
touches the point
x
from both directions, so that
U=V_{\xi}=V_{-\xi}
. This corresponds to the situation in Figure
7.1
(
A
). Then

(7.3)

\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\bigcup_{i\in\{1,2\}}\Big(\,g_{\xi,y_{i}}\big([0,s_{\xi,y_{i}}]\big)\cup g_{-\xi,y_{i}}\big([0,s_{-\xi_{i},y_{i}}]\big)\hskip-1.42262pt\Big).

In this case, we associate a curve segment with each extremal contributor
y_{1},y_{2}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
by
defining for each
i\in\{1,2\}
the continuous maps
\gamma_{x}^{(i)}:\left[0,1\right]\to\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}
through

(7.4)

\gamma_{x}^{(i)}(s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\vskip 0.0ptg_{-\xi,y_{i}}\big(s_{-\xi,y_{i}}-sL^{(i)}\big),&\text{if }s\in\Big[0,\frac{s_{-\xi,y_{i}}}{L^{(i)}}\Big),\\
g_{\xi,y_{i}}\big(sL^{(i)}-s_{-\xi,y_{i}}\big),&\text{if }s\in\Big[\frac{s_{-\xi,y_{i}}}{L^{(i)}},1\Big],\\
\end{array}\right.

where
g_{-\xi,y_{i}},g_{\xi,y_{i}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
, and
L^{(i)}:=s_{\xi,y_{i}}+s_{-\xi,y_{i}}
with
s_{\xi,y_{i}}
and
s_{-\xi,y_{i}}
as in (
7.3
). We thus obtain
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cup\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}
, where
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}:=\gamma_{x}^{(i)}([0,1])
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

(ii)

Let
x\in Q_{2}
. Then
U=V_{\xi}\neq V_{-\xi}
so that the graphs
g([0,s_{-\xi,y_{1}}])
and
g([0,s_{-\xi,y_{2}}])
intersect the local boundary subset
\partial U\cap B_{x}
only at
x
. They are thus not relevant for a local representation of
\partial U
at
x
. This corresponds to the situation in Figure
7.1
(
B
). Consequently, we have

(7.5)

\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=g_{\xi,y_{1}}\big([0,s_{\xi,y_{1}}]\big)\cup g_{\xi,y_{2}}\big([0,s_{\xi,y_{2}}]\big)

for
g_{\xi,y_{1}},g_{\xi,y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
. We thus define

(7.6)

\gamma_{x}(s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\vskip 0.0ptg_{\xi,y_{1}}\big(s_{\xi,y_{1}}-sL\big),&\text{if }s\in\Big[0,\frac{s_{\xi,y_{1}}}{L}\Big),\\
g_{\xi,y_{2}}\big(sL-s_{\xi,y_{1}}\big),&\text{if }s\in\Big[\frac{s_{\xi,y_{1}}}{L},1\Big],\\
\end{array}\right.

where
g_{\xi,y_{1}},g_{\xi,y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
, and
L:=s_{\xi,y_{1}}+s_{\xi,y_{2}}
with
s_{\xi,y_{1}}
and
s_{\xi,y_{2}}
as in (
7.5
). In this case, we obtain a representation using only one curve, since
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}:=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
.

For
x\in Q_{1}
, we define
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}:=\gamma_{x}^{(i)}([0,1])
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, where the functions
\gamma_{x}^{(i)}
are as in (
7.4
). Then
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cup\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}
, and it follows directly from the definition of the functions
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
that the image
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}
is a simple curve for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Note furthermore that sharp-sharp singularities by definition exhibit sharp-type geometry in the direction of both extremal outward directions
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, see Proposition
4.2
(i). Proposition
4.2
thus implies the existence of some
r_{1},r_{2}>0
for which
g_{\xi_{i},y_{1}}(s)\neq g_{\xi_{i},y_{2}}(s)
for all
s\in(0,r_{i})
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. Hence,
\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cap\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}\cap B_{r}(x)=\{x\}
for all
r<\min\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
. By choosing
r<\min\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
for the local boundary representation in the first place, we obtain the equivalent property
\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cap\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}=\{x\}
.

For
x\in Q_{2}
, we define
\Gamma_{x}:=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
, where the function
\gamma_{x}
is as in (
7.6
). The fact that
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}
follows from the definition of the curve
\gamma_{x}
.
Similarly to the above, Proposition
4.2
implies that
g_{\xi,y_{1}}(s)\neq g_{\xi,y_{2}}(s)
for sufficiently small
s\in(0,r)
. It follows that the curve
\Gamma_{x}
is non-self-intersecting in every sufficiently small neighbourhood
B_{x}
.

Step 2.

We divide
\partial U\setminus Q
into the following two subsets:

(7.7)

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle:=\big\{x\in\partial U\,:\,x\,\,\textrm{is a wedge or }x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(x)\big\},

(7.8)

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle:=\big\{x\in\partial U\,:\,x\,\,\textrm{is a sharp or a sharp-chain singularity}\big\}.

According to Corollary
4.3
, chain and chain-chain singularities (types S6 and S7) do not appear on the boundaries of connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, which implies
\partial U=R\cup P\cup Q
. Furthermore, it follows from Theorem
1
that the sets
R
,
P
and
Q
are disjoint.

Assume first that
x\in R
, so that either
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(x)
or
x
is a wedge. Then
\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{(\xi_{1},y_{1}),(\xi_{2},y_{2})\}
, where for
y_{1}=y_{2}
in case
x\in\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(x)
. It follows immediately from Lemma
3.9
and Proposition
3.10
that
\overline{B_{x}}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=\overline{B_{x}}\cap U
, so that

(7.9)

\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=g_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}\big([0,s_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}]\big)\cup g_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}\big([0,s_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}]\big),

where
g_{\xi_{1},y_{1}},g_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
.
By normalising the arguments with
L:=s_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}+s_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}
, one can thus define a function
\gamma_{x}:[0,1]\to\partial U
analogously to (
7.6
), so that
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}:=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
. The fact that
\Gamma_{x}
defines a simple curve in some neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
follows directly from Proposition
3.10
.

Let then
x\in P
, so that
\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})=\{\xi,-\xi\}
for some
\xi\in S^{1}
. In the following, we denote by
U_{r}(x,v)
an open
x
-centered half-ball of radius
r
, oriented in the direction of
v\in S^{1}
, see (
3.1
). For both sharp and sharp-chain singularities, there exists a unique extremal outward direction
v\in\{\xi,-\xi\}
, towards which the singularity exhibits sharp-type geometry, as defined in Proposition
4.2
(i). This means that there exists a unique connected component
V_{\xi}
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
, and
s_{\xi}>0
, for which
U_{s_{\xi}}(x,\xi)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}=U_{s_{\xi}}(x,\xi)\cap V_{\xi}
.

Moreover, in the case of sharp-chain singularities, one can use the local boundary representation (
3.13
) and Proposition
4.2
to show that there exist no connected components
V
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
,
for which
x\in\partial(V\cap U_{r}(x,-\xi))
.
The detailed argument can be found in the proof of Corollary
4.3
. For one-sided sharp singularities, this follows immediately from their definition, which states that for some
\delta>0
, the set
B_{\delta}(x)\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
is a connected set.

Hence, for both sharp and sharp-chain singularities there exists some
r>0
for which

(7.10)

\partial U\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}=g_{\xi,y_{1}}\big([0,s_{\xi,y_{1}}]\big)\cup g_{\xi,y_{2}}\big([0,s_{\xi,y_{2}}]\big),

where
g_{\xi,y_{1}},g_{\xi,y_{2}}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
are as in Proposition
3.5
. One may thus once more define the function
\gamma_{x}:[0,1]\to\partial U
analogously to (
7.6
) by concatenating the images
g_{\xi,y_{i}}\big([0,s_{\xi,y_{i}}]\big)
in (
7.10
) and normalising the argument with
L:=s_{\xi_{1},y_{1}}+s_{\xi_{2},y_{2}}
, so that
\partial U\cap\overline{B_{x}}=\Gamma_{x}:=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
.
∎

It follows from Lemma
7.2
that the boundary
\partial U
of every connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
can be covered by the (infinite) collection

\mathcal{M}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in\partial U\setminus Q_{1}\big\}\cup\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\,:\,x\in Q_{1},\,i\in\{1,2\}\big\}

of simple curves. The compactness of
E
implies that
\partial U
is compact as well, and
\mathcal{M}
thus always has a finite subcover. We show next that
\mathcal{M}
moreover has a finite, order two
1
1

1

A cover
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{\alpha}\,:\,\alpha\in A\}
of a set
X\subset\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}U_{\alpha}
, indexed by the set
A
, is said to be of
order
n
, if the set
A(x):=\{\alpha\in A\,:\,x\in U_{\alpha}\}
contains at most
n
elements for all
x\in X
, and it is said to be
minimal
, if no
U\in\mathcal{U}
may be removed so that
\mathcal{U}\setminus\{U\}
is still a cover for
X
.
subcover
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that contains all the curves
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}
, with
i\in\{1,2\}
, corresponding to sharp-sharp singularities
x\in Q_{1}
.

Lemma 7.3

(Finite subcover of order two)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact, let
\varepsilon>0
and let
U
be a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. In addition, let the functions
\gamma_{x}
,
\gamma_{x}^{(1)},\gamma_{x}^{(2)}:[0,1]\to\partial U
and the corresponding simple curves
\Gamma_{x}:=\gamma_{x}([0,1])
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}:=\gamma_{x}^{(i)}([0,1])
with
i\in\{1,2\}
, be as in Lemma
7.2
.
Then there exists a finite subset
X^{*}\subset\partial U\setminus Q_{1}
for which the collection

(7.11)

\mathcal{M}^{*}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in X^{*}\big\}\cup\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\,:\,x\in Q_{1},\,i\in\{1,2\}\big\}

of simple curves is a finite, minimal, order two cover of
\partial U
.
†
†
footnotemark:

Proof.

Let the sets
R,P\subset\partial U
be as in (
7.7
) and (
7.8
). We now use the curve representations
\Gamma_{x}
for
x\in R\cup P\cup Q_{2}
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}
for
x\in Q_{1}
given by Lemma
7.2
in order to construct an order two cover for the boundary
\partial U
. To begin with, consider the open cover
\mathcal{U}_{0}:=\{B_{x}\,:\,x\in\partial U\}
. Since
\partial U
is compact, there exists a finite subcover
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\{B_{x}\,:\,x\in X_{0}\}\subset\mathcal{U}_{0}
, corresponding to some finite subset
X_{0}\subset\partial U
. We argue that in fact there exists a further subcover
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{U}_{1}
of order two. To see this, consider some boundary point

(7.12)

x\in\partial U\cap B_{x_{1}}\cap B_{x_{2}}\cap B_{x_{3}},

where
B_{x_{k}}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
for
k\in\{1,2,3\}
. We claim that in this situation one of the sets
B_{x_{k}}
can always be removed from the collection so that
\mathcal{U}_{1}\setminus B_{x_{k}}
is still a cover for
\partial U
. As presented above, for each
k\in\{1,2,3\}
the set
X\cap\overline{B_{x_{k}}}
has a parametrisation as the curve
\Gamma_{x_{k}}=\gamma_{x_{k}}([0,1])
. Hence, there exists a continuous bijection
h:[0,1]\to\partial U\cap\bigcup_{k\in\{1,2,3\}}\overline{B_{x_{k}}}
, for which
\partial U\cap B_{x_{k}}=h\left((a_{k},b_{k})\right)
for some open intervals
\left(a_{k},b_{k}\right)\subset[0,1]
and
k\in\{1,2,3\}
. Equation (
7.12
) is then equivalent to

h^{-1}(x)\in\bigcap_{k\in\{1,2,3\}}(a_{k},b_{k}).

On the other hand, regardless of the order in which the points
a_{k},b_{k}
lie on
[0,1]
—as long as
a_{k}<b_{k}
for all
k\in\{1,2,3\}
—one of the intervals
(a_{k},b_{k})
is always contained in the union of the other two, and can therefore be removed without affecting the cover. One can thus remove any redundant balls
B_{x}
from the cover
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and obtain a minimal, order two subcover
\mathcal{U}_{2}
.

To complete the proof, we still need to ensure that none of the balls
B_{x}
corresponding to sharp-sharp singularities
x\in Q_{1}
were removed from the initial cover
\mathcal{U}_{0}
along the pruning process. To this end, note that according to Lemma
5.3
the set
Q_{1}
is finite. This implies that we can assume the radii of the balls
B_{x}
to have been initially chosen sufficiently small such that
B_{x}\cap Q_{1}=\varnothing
for all
x\in\partial U\setminus Q_{1}
and
B_{x}\cap Q_{1}=\{x\}
for
x\in Q_{1}
. This way, the inclusion of every ball
B_{x}
with
x\in Q_{1}
is necessary in any subcover of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

Hence, we have arrived at the desired finite, minimal subcover
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of order two, in the form

(7.13)

\mathcal{M}^{*}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in X^{*}\big\}\cup\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\,:\,x\in Q_{1},\,i\in\{1,2\}\big\},

where the set
X^{*}
is defined by
X^{*}=\{x\in\partial U\setminus Q_{1}\,:\,B_{x}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\}
.
∎

The significance of the order two property of the cover obtained in Lemma
7.3
, as well as the requirement that all the curves corresponding to
x\in Q_{1}
are included in the cover, both stem from the need to ensure that every boundary segment in the cover has a unique successor and predecessor segment. This makes it is possible to construct Jordan curves on the boundary by following the boundary along adjacent curve segments.

Figure 7.3
.
Tracking the boundary
\partial U
along a finite collection of adjacent balls and corresponding simple curves. In (
A
) and (
B
), the points
x
and
x_{S}
correspond to simple curves
\Gamma_{x},\Gamma_{x_{S}}\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
. Here
\Gamma_{x_{S}}
is the successor of
\Gamma_{x}
, and due to minimality of
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, no point
z\in\Gamma_{x}\cap\Gamma_{x_{S}}
belongs to any other curve
\Gamma\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
. It is not ruled out that
x_{S}\in\Gamma_{x}
, as in (
B
).
In (
C
), which is a close-up of Figure
1.2
(
A
), a longer curve is formed by concatenating such overlapping local representations.

Proposition 7.4

(Boundaries of connected components of the complement as finite unions of Jordan curves)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact, let
\varepsilon>0
and let
U
be a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Then
\partial U=\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}J_{i}
, where
M\in\mathbb{N}
and each
J_{i}
is a Jordan curve. The representation is unique up to parametrisation, and for any
i\neq j
, the intersection
J_{i}\cap J_{j}
contains at most one point.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps. First, we use the finite, order two cover given by Lemma
7.3
to construct the Jordan curves on the boundary. We then argue that this representation is unique up to parametrisation, and finish by showing that the intersection of any two Jordan curves in the representation is either a singleton or the empty set. The last two facts are essentially implied by the Jordan Curve Theorem and the connectedness of the set
U
.
2
2

2

The Jordan Curve Theorem states that for any set
J\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is homeomorphic to the unit circle
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus J
has precisely two connected components
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, one of which is bounded and the other unbounded, with the common boundary
\partial A_{1}=\partial A_{2}=J
.

Step 1: Construction of Jordan Curves.
Let

(7.14)

\mathcal{M}^{*}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in X^{*}\big\}\cup\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\,:\,x\in Q_{1},\,i\in\{1,2\}\big\}

be the finite, minimal, order two cover of
\partial U
given by Lemma
7.3
. The sets
X^{*}
and
Q_{1}
are as in (
7.13
), and
\Gamma_{x}
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}
are simple curves. Since
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is minimal and order two, each curve
\Gamma
in the collection

\mathcal{A}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in X^{*}\big\}

intersects exactly two other simple curves

\Gamma_{P},\Gamma_{S}\in\mathcal{M}^{*}\setminus\{\Gamma\},

which we call the
predecessor
and
successor
, respectively. It is possible that
\Gamma_{P}=\Gamma_{S}
.
The parametrisations of individual curves
\Gamma\in\mathcal{A}
can thus be combined to form longer curves, see Figure
7.3
. Some of these eventually loop back onto themselves, forming Jordan curves, while others connect to curves

\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\,:\,x\in Q_{1},\,i\in\{1,2\}\big\}.

By construction,
\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}\cap\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}=\{x\}
for each
x\in Q_{1}
, and in addition, each
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{B}
intersects precisely two other curves

\Gamma_{P},\Gamma_{S}\in\mathcal{M}^{*}\setminus\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(1)},\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}\big\}.

One can thus start from any
\Gamma\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
and follow the boundary along uniquely defined successor and predecessor curves. The order two property and the finiteness of the collection
\mathcal{M}^{*}
ensure that every such extended curve ultimately returns to
\Gamma
without self-intersections, thus forming a Jordan curve. Furthermore, there are only finitely many such curves.

Figure 7.4
.
Schematic illustration of the three ways of representing the boundary
\partial U
near a sharp-sharp singularity
x\in Q_{1}
as the union of two simple curves. Since
I_{i}^{\pm}(x)\subset\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\subset\partial U
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the point
x
must lie at the intersection of two different Jordan curves. In (
A
) the red and blue curves are subsets of the curves
\Gamma_{x}^{(1)}
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(2)}
defined in Lemma
7.2
for
x\in Q_{1}
. In (
B
) the coloured curves bounce back at
x
and in (
C
) they cross at
x
. It turns out that only case (
A
) admits the continuation of such local curves into a representation of
\partial U
as Jordan curves.

Step 2: Uniqueness of Representation.
Assume there exists another representation of
\partial U
as a union of Jordan curves. Since there is only one way to concatenate the curves
\Gamma\in\mathcal{A}
, the only way to obtain a representation that differs from the one constructed in Step 1 is to define the Jordan curves differently at the junction points
x\in Q_{1}
, where four simple curves meet at the same point. For each
x\in Q_{1}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
we write
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}=I_{i}^{+}(x)\cup I_{i}^{-}(x)
where the
I_{i}^{\pm}(x)
are simple curves that intersect each other pairwise only at
x
. The Jordan curves constructed in Step 1 are obtained by arriving to each
x\in Q_{1}
via either
I_{1}^{-}(x)
or
I_{2}^{-}(x)
and leaving along
I_{1}^{+}(x)
or
I_{2}^{+}(x)
, respectively. This corresponds to following either the blue or the red curve in Figure
7.4
(
A
). Assume then that for some
x\in Q_{1}
, a Jordan curve
J
arrives at
x
via
I_{1}^{-}(x)
and leaves via either
I_{2}^{-}(x)
or
I_{2}^{+}(x)
. This corresponds to following the blue curve either in Figure
7.4
(
B
) or in Figure
7.4
(
C
), respectively. The resulting curve would then not coincide with any of the Jordan curves constructed in Step 1. We show that both possibilities lead to a contradiction.

Assume first that
J
bounces back at
x
along
I_{2}^{-}(x)
, as in Figure
7.4
(
B
).
Let
A_{1},A_{2}
be the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus J
, for which
\partial A_{1}=\partial A_{2}=J
.
By definition,
J
has no self-intersections, which implies that it intersects the curves
I_{1}^{+}(x)
and
I_{2}^{+}(x)
only at
x
.
It follows that
J\cap B_{r}(x)=I_{1}^{-}(x)\cup I_{2}^{-}(x)
, where
r>0
is the radius of the local boundary representation at
x
, see Proposition
3.5
.
Consequently,
J
divides the ball neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
into two connected components
C_{1},C_{2}\subset B_{r}(x)
, which satisfy
C_{i}\subset A_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
be the extremal outward direction for which
\partial U\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)=I_{1}^{-}(x)\cup I_{2}^{-}(x)
for some
r>0
. Choose some
x(\xi)\in U\cap U_{r}(x,\xi)
and
x(-\xi)\in U\cap U_{r}(x,-\xi)
. Now, since
U
is connected and open, there exists a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to U
connecting
x(\xi)
and
x(-\xi)
. But this is impossible, because it would imply that
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is connected.

Assume then that
J
crosses
x
along
I_{2}^{+}(x)
, as in Figure
7.4
(
C
). Since
J
cannot cross itself, it follows that another Jordan curve
J_{1}\subset\partial U
contains the curves
I_{2}^{-}(x)
and
I_{1}^{+}(x)
. As before, let
A_{1},A_{2}
be the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus J
, for which
\partial A_{1}=\partial A_{2}=J
. Now
J_{1}\cap A_{i}\neq\varnothing
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
. But this contradicts the fact that the connected set
U
must be a subset of either
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
.

Step 3: Pairwise Intersections.
Assume, contrary to the claim, that there exist two Jordan curves
J_{1},J_{2}\subset\partial U
and some
x_{1},x_{2}\in J_{1}\cap J_{2}
with
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
. We first note that if it were the case that
J_{1}\cap B_{r}(x)=J_{2}\cap B_{r}(x)
for all
x\in Q_{1}
and some
r=r(x)>0
, this would imply
J_{1}=J_{2}
. Therefore, and because
J_{1}\cap J_{2}\neq\varnothing
, there must exist some
x\in Q_{1}\cap(J_{1}\cap J_{2})
for which
J_{1}\cap B_{x}\neq J_{2}\cap B_{x}
. We may assume
x=x_{1}
.
One may then construct a new Jordan curve
J^{*}\subset\partial U
by following
J_{1}
from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
and returning back to
x_{1}
via
J_{2}
.
Note that, because of this rerouting of the curve, the local geometry of
J^{*}
around
x_{1}
corresponds to either case (
B
) or (
C
) in Figure
7.4
.
Let
A_{1},A_{2}
be the connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus J^{*}
, for which
\partial A_{1}=\partial A_{2}=J^{*}
. Since
U
is connected, either
U\subset A_{1}
or
U\subset A_{2}
. But this contradicts the fact that
J^{*}
divides the neighbourhood
B_{r}(x_{1})
into two disjoint connected components
C_{1}\subset A_{1}
and
C_{2}\subset A_{2}
, for which
C_{i}\cap U\neq\varnothing
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.
∎

We now prove the main result of this chapter, Theorem
5
, by combining Proposition
7.4
and Corollary
4.3
.

Theorem 5
.

For a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
, the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is a disjoint union

\partial E_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{I}\cup J,

where
\mathcal{I}
is the set of inaccessible singularities and
J=\bigcup_{i\in I}J_{i}
is a countable (possibly finite) union of Jordan curves
J_{i}
. Furthermore, there is a unique representation with the property that each Jordan curve
J_{i}
satisfies
J_{i}\subset\partial U
for some connected component
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{U}
the collection of connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Corollary
4.3
asserts that each boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
lies on the boundary
\partial U
of some
U\in\mathcal{U}
if and only if it is not a chain (type S6) or chain-chain (type S7) singularity. Proposition
7.4
then implies that

(7.15)

\partial E_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{I}\cup\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{N_{U}}J_{n}(U)\bigg),

where
N_{U}\in\mathbb{N}
for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
J_{n}(U)\subset\partial U
is a Jordan curve for all
1\leq n\leq N_{U}
. For each
U\in\mathcal{U}
, the collection
\{J_{n}(U)\,:\,n\in N_{U}\}
is furthermore unique up to parametrisation.

Due to the compactness of
E_{\varepsilon}
, the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
contains only one unbounded connected component. All other connected components of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
are contained in some ball
B_{M}(0)
, centered at the origin, with radius
M<\infty
. Since they are open, each of them contains an open ball. Hence, the Lebesgue measure of each such component
U
satisfies
m(U)>0
. However, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there can be only finitely many
U
with
m(U)>1/n
. It follows from this that there are at most countably many connected components of the complement, which also implies that the number of Jordan curves
J_{n}(U)
in (
7.15
) is countable.
∎

Chapter 8
Uniform Rectifiability and Ahlfors Regularity

In this chapter we recall the definitions of rectifiability and Ahlfors regularity, and show that the Jordan curve subsets of the boundary are
uniformly rectifiable.
We also give an example of an
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood whose boundary is not an Ahlfors-regular set.

Recall that a
curve
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the image of a closed interval under some continuous map
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, see Definition
7.1
.
The
length
of a curve
\Gamma
is given by

\mathcal{L}(\Gamma):=\sup\sum_{k=1}^{m}|\gamma(t_{k})-\gamma(t_{k-1})|,

where the supremum is taken over all finite subsets
\{t_{0},\ldots,t_{m}\}
of the interval
[a,b]
. We say that
\Gamma
is a
rectifiable curve
, if
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)<\infty
. Equivalently,
\Gamma
is a rectifiable curve if there exists some Lipschitz map
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
for which
\Gamma=f([a,b])
.
The notion of a
rectifiable set

[
DS93
,
Mat95
]
generalises the above definition.

Definition 8.1

(
1
-rectifiable set)
.

A set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
1
-rectifiable
(or simply
rectifiable
), if
\mathcal{H}^{1}(A)<\infty
and there exists a countable family of Lipschitz functions
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which
\mathcal{H}^{1}\big(A\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}(\mathbb{R})\big)=0
.

It was shown in
[
RW10
, Proposition 2.3]
that for any bounded set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
d\geq 1
, both
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and
\partial E_{<\varepsilon}
are
(d-1)
-rectifiable sets.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
x\in A
. The limit

(8.1)

\Theta(A,x):=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(A\cap B_{r}(x))}{2r},

when it exists, is called the (
1
-dimensional)
\emph{density}
of
A
at
x
. A basic result in geometric measure theory is that if
\mathcal{H}^{1}(A)<\infty
, then
A
is rectifiable if and only if the density (
8.1
) exists at almost all
x\in A
, and is equal to
1
. Some authors call sets with this property regular sets. The slightly different notion of Ahlfors-regularity quantifies in a scale-free fashion the Hausdorff-dimensionality of the set
A
.

Definition 8.2

(Ahlfors regularity)
.

A set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
Ahlfors-regular
(or simply
regular
) with dimension
n
, if
A
is closed and there exist constants
a,b>0
so that

(8.2)

ar^{n}\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}\big(A\cap B_{r}(x)\big)\leq br^{n}

for all
x\in A
and
0<r<\textrm{diam}(A)
.
1
1

1

The requirement
r<\textrm{diam}(A)
allows for the lower bound in (
8.2
) to be satisfied with some constant
a>0
also for sets
A
that have finite
n
-dimensional measure.

The notion of
uniform rectifiability

[
DS93
]
refers to a family of closely related geometric properties that, when satisfied by some set
A
, guarantee the boundedness of certain integral operators defined on the space
L^{2}(A)
of square-integrable functions.
A thorough review of uniform rectifiability is provided in the introductory chapter of
[
DS93
]
. We take as the definition the following characterisation, whcih is given in
[
Mat95
, Section 15.23]
.

Definition 8.3

(Uniform rectifiability)
.

A set
A
is
uniformly rectifiable
, if (i) it is closed, (ii) it is Ahlfors regular and (iii) there exists some curve
\Gamma
and some
C>0
for which
A\subset\Gamma
and
\Gamma
satisfies for all
x\in\Gamma
and all
r>0
the inequality

(8.3)

\mathcal{H}^{1}\big(\Gamma\cap B_{r}(x)\big)\leq Cr.

We begin by showing that for any compact
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the Jordan curve subsets of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
are Ahlfors regular.

Proposition 8.4

(Ahlfors regularity of Jordan curve boundary subsets)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact,
\varepsilon>0
and let
U
be a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. Let
J
be a Jordan curve subset of the boundary
\partial U
. Then
J
is Ahlfors regular.

Proof.

The idea is to first identify a suitable ’anchor’ radius
r_{0}>0
which will depend on the particular set
E
and radius
\varepsilon
. After this, we establish the required constants
a,b>0
for the lower and upper bounds in (
8.2
) by considering separately the cases
r<r_{0}
and
r\geq r_{0}
.

Let
J
be a Jordan curve subset of the boundary
\partial U
, where
U
is a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. The appropriate anchor radius
r_{0}
can be defined on the basis of the finite, minimal, order two cover of
J
, whose existence was shown in Lemma
7.3
. In (
8.4
) below, the sets
X^{*}
and
Q_{1}
and the simple curves
\Gamma_{x}
and
\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}
are as in (
7.13
). According to Lemma
7.3
, the collection

(8.4)

\mathcal{M}^{*}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in X^{*}\big\}\cup\big\{\Gamma_{x}^{(i)}\,:\,x\in Q_{1},\,i\in\{1,2\}\big\}

is a finite, minimal, order two cover of the Jordan curve
J
. Since
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is minimal and order two, each curve
\Gamma
in the collection
\mathcal{A}:=\big\{\Gamma_{x}\,:\,x\in X^{*}\big\}

intersects exactly two other simple curves in
\mathcal{M}^{*}\setminus\{\Gamma\}
.
The key observation is that since the union
X^{*}\cup Q_{1}
is finite, there exists some radius
r_{0}>0
with the following property: for any boundary point
x\in J
, there exists a corresponding curve
\Gamma_{x}\in\mathcal{A}
for which
J\cap B_{r}(x)=\Gamma_{x}\cap B_{r}(x)
whenever
r\leq r_{0}
. We now identify the constants
a,b>0
for the lower and upper bounds in (
8.2
)

Lower bound.
Let
r_{0}
be as defined above. Let
x\in J
, and consider first some radius
r\leq r_{0}
. Then, as argued above, there exists some curve
\Gamma_{x}\in\mathcal{A}
for which
J\cap B_{r}(x)=\Gamma_{x}\cap B_{r}(x)
.
Note that each
\Gamma\in\mathcal{A}
is constructed from the graphs in a local boundary representation around some
z\in J
, see Lemma
7.2
and Figure
7.2
. The set
\Gamma_{x}\cap B_{r}(x)
thus consists of two disjoint, continuous curves that connect the center point
x
of the
r
-radius ball
B_{r}(x)
to its circumference
\partial B_{r}(x)
. From this we obtain the lower bound

(8.5)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r}(x))=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{x}\cap B_{r}(x))\geq 2r.

Assume then that
r_{0}\leq r\leq\textrm{diam}(J)
. In this case we have

(8.6)

\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r}(x))}{r}\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r_{0}}(x))}{\textrm{diam}(J)}\geq\frac{2r_{0}}{\textrm{diam}(J)}.

Setting
a:=\textrm{min}\big(2,2r_{0}/\textrm{diam}(J)\big)
, the lower bounds (
8.5
) and (
8.6
)
imply

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r}(x))\geq ar

for all
r\in\big(0,\textrm{diam}(J)\big)
.

Upper bound.

Let
r_{0}
be as defined above. Let
x\in J
and consider first the case
r<r_{0}
. Then, as argued for the lower bound above,
J\cap B_{r}(x)=\Gamma_{x}\cap B_{r}(x)
for some
\Gamma_{x}\in\mathcal{A}
. Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
. By construction, the curve
\Gamma_{x}
is the union of either two or four curves that are obtained as images
g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{\xi,s}])
under Lipschitz-continuous functions
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
, where
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
are extremal pairs and
0<s_{\xi,s}\leq r_{0}
for each
(\xi,y)
.
According to Proposition
3.6
, the functions
g_{\xi,y}
are
2/\sqrt{3}
Lipschitz, so that

(8.7)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r}(x))=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma_{x}\cap B_{r}(x))\leq\sum_{j=1}^{4}\frac{2r}{\sqrt{3}}=\frac{8r}{\sqrt{3}}.

Assume then that
r\geq r_{0}
. In this case we have

(8.8)

\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r}(x))}{r}\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)}{r_{0}}.

Note that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})<\infty
and define
b:=\max\big\{8/\sqrt{3},\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)/r_{0}\big\}
.
2
2

2

The finiteness of the length of the boundary is one of the very first properties established about
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods, see
[
Erd45
, Section 6]
.
Thus, it follows from the estimates (
8.7
) and (
8.8
) that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap B_{r}(x))\leq br

for all
r\in\big(0,\textrm{diam}(J)\big)
.
∎

We next leverage Proposition
8.4
in order to show that for the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
of the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of any compact
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}

there exists some curve
\Gamma
that
satisfies the upper bound (
8.3
) for some constant
C>0
.

Lemma 8.5

(Curve covering of the boundary)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists a curve
\Gamma\supset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
and some constant
C>0
for which the upper bound (
8.3
) is satisfied for all
r>0
and all
x\in\Gamma
.

Proof.

Since
E
is compact, there exists a finite collection of compact sets
A_{k}
with diameter at most
\varepsilon/2
and for which

E=\bigcup_{k}A_{k}
. According to
[
Bro72
, Lemma 1]
the boundary of each
A_{k}
is a Jordan curve
J_{k}
. It then follows from Proposition
8.4
that there exist constants
C_{k}
for which

(8.9)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{k}\cap B_{r}(x))\leq C_{k}r

for all
x\in J_{k}
and
0<r\leq\textrm{diam}(J_{k})
. In fact, we may assume that (
8.9
) is valid for
0<r\leq\textrm{diam}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
for all
k
, see (
8.8
).
The individual Jordan curves
J_{k}
can be arranged into a sequence in which consecutive Jordan curves are connected to each other by a straight line segment
L_{k}
. The union
\bigcup_{k}\big(J_{k}\cup L_{k}\big)
thus forms a curve
\Gamma
for which
\bigcup_{k}J_{k}\subset\Gamma
. According to
[
Bro72
, Theorem 1]
,
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\subset\bigcup_{k}J_{k}
so that
\Gamma
