covers the boundary. Since only one connecting line segment is needed for each Jordan curve, it follows that a ball neighbourhood
B_{r}(x)
with
r>0
around any
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
intersects at most
N=\textrm{card}\big(\{J_{k}\}\big)
line segments. The same holds for the Jordan curves
J_{k}
, so that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B_{r}(x))\leq rN\big(\max_{k}\{C_{k}\}+2\big).\qed

Note that Lemma
8.5
makes no assumptions regarding the types of singularities that may appear on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. From this, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 6

(Sufficient condition for uniform rectifiability)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact, let
\varepsilon>0
, and assume that the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
contains no chain or chain-chain singularities. Then
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly rectifiable.

Proof.

Since
E
is compact and the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is assumed to contain no chain singularities, there must exist only finitely many Jordan curve subsets
J_{k}\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. In addition, every boundary point lies on some
J_{k}
. According to Proposition
8.4
, each
J_{k}
is Ahlfors regular with its associated lower and upper bound constants
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
in (
8.2
). As argued in the proof of the previous lemma, the constants
b_{k}
can be assumed to hold for all
0<r\leq\textrm{diam}(\partial E_{\varepsilon})
for all
k
, see (
8.8
).
Since there are only finitely many Jordan curves, we may define
a:=\min_{k}\{a_{k}\}
and
b:=\max_{k}\{b_{k}\}
. It follows that
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is Ahlfors regular. Since it is closed, the claim now follows from Lemma
8.5
.
∎

8.1.
Counterexample to Uniform Rectifiability

We now construct an example of an
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood
E_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
fails to satisfy the lower bound in the Ahlfors regularity condition (
8.2
) at a chain singularity. We obtain the underlying set
E
as the union of two copies of a point sequence on the positive real line, embedded in the plane at a distance of
2\varepsilon
from each other.

Example 8.6
.

Define the sequences
(a_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(b_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
by setting
a_{n}:=2^{-n^{2}}
and
b_{n}:=2a_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
A:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[b_{n},a_{n-1}]
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
and define

\displaystyle E^{+}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(x,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,x\in A\cup[-1,0]\big\},

\displaystyle E^{-}

\displaystyle:=\big\{(x,-\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,x\in A\cup[-1,0]\big\}.

Finally, let
E=E^{+}\cup E^{-}
and consider the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood
E_{\varepsilon}
. The interval
[-1,0]
is included in the definition of the sets
E^{\pm}
above for simplicity, so that the part of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
that intersects small neighbourhoods of the origin
(0,0)
lies entirely on the right half plane.

We now show that the origin is a chain singularity on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, and that the lower bound in the Ahlfors regularity condition (
8.2
) fails at this point. To this end, consider a ball neighbourhood
B(r):=B_{r}\big((0,0)\big)
of radius
r>0
around the origin, and let
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B(r)
be some boundary point inside this neighbourhood. Then
x\in\partial B_{\varepsilon}(z)
for some
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in E
, in which
z_{1}\in[0,r]
and
z_{2}\in\{\varepsilon,-\varepsilon\}
. Hence, as
r
decreases, the tangential direction of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
at
x
approaches horizontal, which implies in particular that the absolute values of the (one-sided) derivatives of the local boundary representation are bounded from above by some
K(r)>0
with
\lim_{r\to 0}K(r)=0
. It follows that the Hausdorff measure of the boundary subset
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B(r)
satisfies the upper bound

(8.10)

\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B(r)\right)\leq 2K(r)\ell\big([0,r]\setminus A\big),

in which
\ell\big([0,r]\setminus A\big)
is the length of the one-dimensional set
[0,r]\setminus A\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We now show that

\liminf_{r\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap B(r)\right)}{r}=0,

which implies that
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is not Ahlfors regular. Due to (
8.10
), it suffices to show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\ell\big([0,a_{n}]\setminus A\big)}{a_{n}}=0.

Note first that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(8.11)

\displaystyle\ell\big([0,a_{n}]\setminus A\big)

\displaystyle=a_{n}-\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\ell\big([b_{k+1},a_{k}]\big)=\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\ell\big([a_{k},b_{k}]\big)=\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}a_{k}.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the ratio of consecutive terms satisfies
R_{n}:=a_{n+1}/a_{n}=2^{-1-2n}
, so that
\lim_{n\to\infty}R_{n}=0
and

\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}a_{k}=2^{-(n+1)^{2}}\big(1+R_{n+1}+R_{n+1}R_{n+2}+\ldots\big)<2^{-(n+1)^{2}}\frac{R_{n+1}}{1-R_{n+1}}.

This implies

\frac{\ell\big([0,a_{n}]\setminus A\big)}{a_{n}}=\frac{2^{-(n+1)^{2}}}{2^{-n^{2}}}\frac{R_{n+1}}{1-R_{n+1}}=\frac{R_{n+1}^{2}}{1-R_{n+1}}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
, which completes the argument.

Example
8.6
shows that boundaries of
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods that contain chain singularities are generally not uniformly rectifiable. However, it is also easy to construct examples of boundaries of
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods that do satisfy the lower bound in (
8.2
) even though they contain chain singularities. The mere existence of chain singularities therefore does not in itself rule out the possibility that the boundary of an
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood is uniformly rectifiable. Thus, in light of Theorem
6
, the uniform rectifiability of the boundary hinges on whether the lower bound in the Ahlfors regularity condition (
8.2
) is satisfied at the chain singularities appearing on the boundary.

Chapter 9
Curvature

In this final chapter we show that curvature is defined almost everywhere on Jordan curves
J\subset\partial U\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, where
U
is some connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. It is not clear from the outset that curvature can be defined on these sets even almost everywhere. It was shown in Example
5.5
that shallow singularities (types S4 and S5), which are defined as accumulation points of increasingly shallow wedge-type singularities, may lie densely on boundary segments that have positive one-dimensional Hausdorff measure. It follows that even though Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
provide a local representation of the boundary as Lipschitz-continuous functions, the first derivatives of these function generally exist only almost everywhere.

However, since there are no cusp singularities on the boundary, the tangent function on the boundary
\partial U
may only have jumps in one direction. In the opposite direction, the rate of change of the tangential direction is bounded from below by the curvature of an
\varepsilon
-radius ball, since every boundary point
x\in\partial U
lies on the boundary of such a ball. We use these properties to show that the one-sided tangent functions are locally of bounded variation, and therefore have a derivative at almost every point. One can then use these to obtain the (signed) curvature of the boundary.

9.1.
Existence of Curvature via Bounded Variation

Let
U
be a connected component of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
and let
J\subset\partial U
be one of its Jordan curve subsets, see Theorem
5
. In general,
J
has a well-defined tangent only at almost every point. However, Proposition
2.14
asserts that the directional (left and right) tangents coincide with extremal outward directions, which exist at every
x\in J
according to Proposition
2.12
. Lemma
7.3
asserts that
J
can be covered by a finite collection of simple curves that are constructed from the graphs of local boundary representations, given by Proposition
3.5
. Hence, in order to establish the existence of curvature on the Jordan curve
J
, it suffices to work with these local representations.

In this section, we prove the final main result of the paper, Theorem
7
, which states that curvature is well-defined almost everywhere on the Jordan curve subsets
J
of the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
. To prove the theorem, we apply a criterion for bounded variation to the local boundary representations of
J
. We present here the proof of the main theorem, and postpone the more technical proof of the criterion to Section
9.2
.

9.1.1.
Boundary Points in a Local Coordinate System

A local boundary representation at
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is a finite collection
\mathcal{G}(x)
of continuous functions
g_{\xi,y}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for some
0<r<\varepsilon/2
, one for each extremal pair
1
1

1

For each boundary point
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}
, the set of
extremal pairs

\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
consists of all the pairs
(\xi,y)
of extremal outward directions
\xi\in\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
and extremal contributors
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
for which
\langle x-y,\xi\rangle=0
.

(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, with the property that

(9.1)

\partial E_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}=\bigcup_{(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})}g_{\xi,y}\left(A_{\xi,y}\right)

for some closed sets
A_{\xi,y}\subset[0,r]
, see Proposition
3.5
. More precisely, for each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
there exists a continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,r]\to\mathbb{R}
for which

(9.2)

g_{\xi,y}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}.

According to Corollary
4.3
, chain singularities do not appear on the boundary
\partial U
for connected components
U
of the complement
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{\varepsilon}
. It follows then from Proposition
3.5
that for each
x\in\partial U
, the sets
A_{\xi,y}
in (
9.1
) are intervals
A_{\xi,y}=[0,s_{\xi,y}]
for some
s_{\xi,y}\in[0,r]
.

Figure 9.1
.
The relationship between the tangential direction
\xi_{s}^{+}
, the slope
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)
and the extremal contributor
y_{s}^{+}
at
x_{s}=g_{\xi,y}(s)=\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)
for
s\in[0,r]
. Here
a_{s}^{+}<0
, and the slope at
x_{s}
satisfies
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)=-a_{s}^{+}/b_{s}^{+}=-a_{s}^{+}/\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-(a_{s}^{+})^{2}}=:p\big(a_{s}^{+}\big)
.

Consider a fixed
x\in J
and an extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. To facilitate the subsequent analysis, we choose local coordinates given by the orthogonal unit vectors
\xi
and
(x-y)/\varepsilon
, and place the origin of this coordinate system at
x
. However, to simplify notation, we assume without loss of generality that this coordinate system coincides with the standard Euclidean coordinate system, so that
x=(0,0)
,
\xi=(1,0)
and
(x-y)/\varepsilon=(0,1)
. For each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
, we define

x_{s}:=g_{\xi,y}(s)=\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)\in J,

where
f^{\xi,y}:[0,s_{\xi,y}]\to\mathbb{R}
is as in (
9.2
). The boundary subset
J\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}
can thus be expressed as the union of graphs

J\cap\overline{B_{r}(x)}=\bigcup_{(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})}\big\{\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)\,:\,s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]\big\},

where each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
defines its corresponding local coordinates.

According to Proposition
3.1
, for each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
the extremal contributors
y_{s}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s})
satisfy
y_{s}\in B_{\varepsilon/2}(y)
, where
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
is the extremal contributor corresponding to the extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, see Proposition
3.5
. This implies that for each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
the extremal outward directions
\xi_{s}\in\Xi_{x_{s}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
deviate only slightly from the
\xi
-axis in the local coordinate system. We call them the right and left extremal outward direction at
x_{s}
, and denote them by
\xi_{s}^{+}
and
\xi_{s}^{-}
. According to Proposition
2.14
, these coincide with the tangential directions at
x_{s}\in J
. In the local coordinates, the slopes of the one-sided tangents of the graph
\big\{\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)\,:\,s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]\big\}
are given by the the left and right derivatives

(9.3)

D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s):=\lim_{h\to\pm 0}\frac{f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)}{h}.

These are related to the extremal outward directions
\xi_{s}^{\pm}=\big(\xi_{1}^{\pm}(s),\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s)\big)\in\Xi_{x_{s}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
through

(9.4)

D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)=\frac{\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s)}{\xi_{1}^{\pm}(s)},

see Figure
9.1
.

9.1.2.
A Condition for Bounded Variation

In order to prove the existence of curvature almost everywhere on
J
, we consider a local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
around an arbitrary boundary point
x\in J
and show that for each extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, the second derivative of the function
f^{\xi,y}
in (
9.2
) exists almost everywhere on the interval
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
. We begin by stating the following general criterion for bounded variation that applies to bounded functions on a closed interval.

Lemma 9.1

(A criterion for bounded variation)
.

Let let
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded function and assume there exists some
q>0
for which

(9.5)

f(s+h)-f(s)\geq-qh

for all
s\in[a,b)
and all
h>0
that satisfy
s+h\in[a,b]
. Then
f
is of bounded variation on
[a,b]
.

Proof.

Since
f
is bounded, there exists some
m>0
for which
f(s)\in[-m,m]
for all
s\in[a,b]
. Assume now contrary to the claim that
f
is not of bounded variation.
Then there exists a partition
a=s_{0}<s_{1}<\ldots<s_{K}=b
of the interval
[a,b]
for which

(9.6)

S:=\sum_{j=1}^{K}|f(s_{j})-f(s_{j-1})|>2(p+m),

where
p:=\max\{q(b-a),2m\}
.
Consider the index sets

\displaystyle J^{+}

\displaystyle:=\big\{j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\,:\,f(s_{j})-f(s_{j-1})>0\big\},

\displaystyle J^{-}

\displaystyle:=\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus J^{+},

that correspond to contributions from positive and negative differences in (
9.6
), respectively.
Then
S=S^{+}-S^{-}
, where

(9.7)

S^{+}:=\sum_{j\in J_{+}}\big(f(s_{j})+f(s_{j-1})\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad S^{-}:=\sum_{j\in J^{-}}\big(f(s_{j})-f(s_{j-1})\big).

It follows from (
9.5
) that
S^{-}\geq-q(b-a)\geq-p
, which together with (
9.6
) implies the lower bound
S^{+}>p+2m
. Combining these bounds leads to the contradiction

\displaystyle f(b)

\displaystyle=f(a)+S^{+}+S^{-}>-m+(p+2m)-p=m.\qed

The main step towards proving Theorem
7
is to show that the one-sided derivatives
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}
satisfy condition (
9.5
) of Lemma
9.1
for a certain
q>0
and are thus of bounded variation on the interval
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
.

Proposition 9.2

(A lower bound for differences of right derivatives)
.

Let
E\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a compact set and let
x\in J\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
where
J
is a Jordan curve. Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
, where each
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
satisfies

g_{\xi,y}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\varepsilon^{-1}(x-y)

for some continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,s_{\xi,y}]\to\mathbb{R}
. Then the left and right derivatives
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}
satisfy the inequality

(9.8)

D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)\geq-\frac{8h}{3\sqrt{3}\varepsilon}

for all
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
and
s,h>0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
.

The proof of Proposition
9.2
is the most technical part of the proof of Theorem
7
, and is presented separately in Section
9.2
. We now proceed to combine Proposition
9.2
and Lemma
9.1
into a general statement about the existence of curvature on the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{1}
the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure, and by
\mathcal{I}
the set of inaccessible singularities, see Corollary
4.3
.

Theorem 7

(Existence of curvature)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be compact and let
\varepsilon>0
. Then for
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-almost all
x\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{I}
, the (signed) curvature
\kappa
exists and is given by the formula

(9.9)

\kappa(s_{x})=\frac{\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}f^{\xi,y}(s_{x})}{\left(1+\left(\frac{d}{ds}f^{\xi,y}(s_{x})\right)^{2}\right)^{3/2}},

where the coordinates
s_{x}
and
f^{\xi,y}(s_{x})
are associated with a local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(z)
at some
z\in\partial E_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{I}
, such that
x=z+s_{x}\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s_{x})(z-y)/\varepsilon
for some
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{z}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

According to Theorem
5
,
\partial E_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{I}
is a countable union
\mathcal{J}
of Jordan curves. Since each
J\in\mathcal{J}
is compact, there exists a finite collection
Z
of points
z\in J
and corresponding boundary representations
\mathcal{G}(z)
, for which

J=\bigcup_{z\in Z}\left(\bigcup_{(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{z}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})}g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{\xi,y}])\right).

The closed intervals
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
are as in Proposition
3.5
. It suffices to show that for each
z\in Z
, curvature exists outside a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set on each curve
g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{\xi,y}])
, where
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{z}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
.

Let
z\in Z
and let
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(z)
for some
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{z}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
. According to Proposition
3.5
there exists a continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,s_{\xi,y}]\to\mathbb{R}
for which

g_{\xi,y}(s)=z+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\frac{z-y}{\varepsilon}.

Since the one-sided derivatives
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)
are related to the extremal outward directions
\xi_{s}^{\pm}
via (
9.4
), it follows that
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)=D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s)
whenever
\xi_{s}^{+}=-\xi_{s}^{-}
. This implies that the one-sided derivatives agree on
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
apart from an at most countably infinite set, since it follows from Theorem
2
and Corollary
4.3
that the set

\displaystyle J\setminus\big\{x\in J\,:\,\Xi_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\{\xi,-\xi\}\,\,\textrm{for some}\,\,\xi\in S^{1}\big\}

\displaystyle=J\setminus\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E),

where
\mathrm{Unp_{\varepsilon}}(E)
is as in Definition
2.1
, is at most countably infinite.

We next confirm that the assumptions of Lemma
9.1
are satisfied for the left and right derivatives
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)
on the interval
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
. By the definition of a local boundary representation, the extremal contributors
y_{s}^{\pm}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s})
satisfy
y_{s}^{\pm}\in B_{\varepsilon/2}(y)
, where
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
corresponds to the extremal pair
(\xi,y)
. This imposes an upper bound on the angle between the
\xi
-axis and the corresponding extremal outward directions
\xi_{s}^{\pm}\in\Xi_{x_{s}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, which in turn implies a bound on the left and right derivatives
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}
on
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
through the relationship (
9.4
). Due to inequality (
9.8
) in Proposition
9.2
, one can thus apply Lemma
9.1
, which implies that the one-sided derivatives
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}
are of bounded variation on
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
.

Thus, each of the functions
D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}
has a (two-sided) derivative
\frac{d}{ds}D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}
almost everywhere on
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
.
2
2

2

Every function of bounded variation can be written as the difference of two non-decreasing functions, and consequently has a finite derivative at almost every point. For details, see for instance
[
KF75
, p. 331]
Since
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}=D^{-}f^{\xi,y}
outside a countable set, this furthermore implies
\frac{d}{ds}D^{+}f^{\xi,y}=\frac{d}{ds}D^{-}f^{\xi,y}
almost everywhere. Consequently, apart from a set
W\subset[0,s_{\xi,y}]
of zero Hausdorff measure, both the first and second (two-sided) derivatives of
f^{\xi,y}
exist, and define curvature on
the graph
\big\{\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)\,:\,s\in[0,s_{\xi,s}]\big\}
via (
9.9
).

According to Proposition
3.6
, the function
s\mapsto\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)
is
2/\sqrt{3}
-Lipschitz on
[0,s_{\xi,y}]
. This implies
\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(f^{\xi,y}(W)\right)\leq 2\mathcal{H}^{1}(W)/\sqrt{3}=0
, since the Hausdorff measure of a Lipschitz transformation is bounded from above by the corresponding Lipschitz constant, see
[
AFP00
, Prop. 2.49]
. It follows that curvature exists outside a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set on each curve
g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{\xi,y}])
.
∎

9.2.
A Lower Bound for Differences of Tangential Directions

In this section we prove Proposition
9.2
, which represents the key step in the proof of Theorem
7
. Proposition
9.2
expresses the geometric observation that it is impossible for the boundary
\partial E_{\varepsilon}
to curve inwards more than a certain threshold, implied by the radius
\varepsilon>0
.

Before proceeding with the proof, we establish some notation. We consider the local boundary representation
\mathcal{G}(x)
around
x\in J
, where
J\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
is a Jordan curve component of the boundary. We work in the local coordinates corresponding to an extremal pair
(\xi,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{x}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
, as defined in Section
9.1.1
. For each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
, define

x_{s}:=g_{\xi,y}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\frac{x-y}{\varepsilon}=\big(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\big)\in J,

where
f^{\xi,y}:[0,s_{\xi,y}]\to\mathbb{R}
is as in (
9.2
). Each of the extremal contributors

y_{s}^{\pm}=\big(y_{1}^{\pm}(s),y_{2}^{\pm}(s)\big)\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s})

lies at the center of an
\varepsilon
-radius circle
3
3

3

Due to the orientation of the extremal contributor
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
relative to
x
, these circles lie below the graph
g_{\xi,y}([0,s_{\xi,y}])
in the
\big(\xi,(x-y)/\varepsilon\big)
-coordinates, and are thus uniquely defined for all
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
.

B_{\varepsilon}\big(y_{s}^{\pm}\big)
, whose tangent at
x_{s}\in\partial B_{\varepsilon}\big(y_{s}^{\pm}\big)
coincides with the respective extremal outward direction

\xi_{s}^{\pm}=\big(\xi_{1}^{\pm}(s),\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s)\big)\in\Xi_{x_{s}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon}),

see Definition
2.6
and Proposition
2.14
. Hence, for each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
,

(9.10)

x_{s}-y_{s}^{\pm}=\big({-\varepsilon}\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s),\varepsilon\xi_{1}^{\pm}(s)\big)=:\big(a_{s}^{\pm},b_{s}^{\pm}\big).

On the other hand, as indicated in (
9.4
), the slopes of the one-sided tangents at
x_{s}
satisfy

(9.11)

D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)=\frac{\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s)}{\xi_{1}^{\pm}(s)}=\frac{\varepsilon\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\big[\varepsilon\xi_{2}^{\pm}(s)\big]^{2}}}=\frac{-a_{s}^{\pm}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\big[a_{s}^{\pm}\big]^{2}}}=:p\big(a_{s}^{\pm}\big).

We call the function
p:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
in (
9.11
), given by
p(s):=-s/\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-s^{2}}
, the
slope function
.

In order to prove Proposition
9.2
, we establish for all
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
and
h>0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
the double inequality

(9.12)

D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-D^{\pm}f^{\xi,y}(s)\geq p\left(a_{s}^{\pm}+h\right)-p\left(a_{s}^{\pm}\right)\geq-\frac{8h}{3\sqrt{3}\varepsilon},

where the coordinates
a_{s}^{\pm}
are defined in (
9.10
). We initially prove (
9.12
) for the right derivative
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}
, and deduce from this the analogous inequality also for the left derivative
D^{-}f^{\xi,y}
. Since
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)=p\big(a_{s}^{+}\big)
, the key to showing inequality (
9.12
) is to demonstrate that
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)\geq p(a_{s}^{+}+h)
whenever
s,h\geq 0
and
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
. We divide the proof into the following steps, of which the first three correspond to Lemmas
9.3
–
9.5
and step (iv) is included in the proof of Proposition
9.2
below:

(i)

identify a lower bound
k(T,h)
for
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
in terms of
h
and the difference

T=T(s,h):=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s);

(ii)

show that the bound
k_{h}(T):=k(T,h)
obtained in step (i) is increasing in
T
for a fixed
h
;

(iii)

show that there exists
\widehat{T}:=\widehat{T}(s,h)\leq T(s,h)
for which
k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)=p(a_{s}^{+}+h)
;

(iv)

combine steps (i)–(iii) to obtain the inequality

D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)\geq k(T,h)\geq k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)=p(a_{s}^{+}+h).

We begin by establishing a lower bound
k(T,h)
for
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
in terms of
T:=T(s,h)
and the
T
-dependent distances

(9.13)

P(T):=\frac{\sqrt{h^{2}+T^{2}}}{2}\quad\textrm{and}\quad A(T):=\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-P^{2}(T)}=\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{h^{2}+T^{2}}{4}}.

Lemma 9.3

(The functional form of the lower bound for the right derivative)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a compact set,
\varepsilon>0
and let
x\in J\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
where
J
is a Jordan curve. Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
, where each
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
satisfies

g_{\xi,y}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\varepsilon^{-1}(x-y)

for some continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,s_{\xi,y}]\to\mathbb{R}
. Then for all
s,h\geq 0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
, the right derivative
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}
satisfies the inequality

(9.14)

D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)\geq k(T,h):=\frac{TA(T)-hP(T)}{hA(T)+TP(T)},

where
T:=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
and the distances
P(T),A(T)
are as in (
9.13
).

Proof.

For each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
we write
a_{s}^{+}:=-\varepsilon\xi_{2}^{+}(s)
and
b_{s}^{+}:=\varepsilon\xi_{1}^{+}(s)
, where
\xi_{s}^{+}=\big(\xi_{1}^{+}(s),\xi_{2}^{+}(s)\big)
is the right extremal outward direction at
x_{s}
.
Fix then some
s,h\geq 0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
and consider the corresponding boundary points
x_{s},x_{s+h}\in J
whose local coordinates are given by

(9.15)

x_{s}:=\left(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\right),\quad x_{s+h}:=\left(s+h,f^{\xi,y}(s+h)\right).

It follows from (
9.10
) that the right extremal contributors
y_{s}^{+}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s})
and
y_{s+h}^{+}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s+h})
satisfy

(9.16)

\displaystyle y_{s}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left(s-a_{s}^{+},f^{\xi,y}(s)-b_{s}^{+}\right),

\displaystyle y_{s+h}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left(s+h-a_{s+h}^{+},f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-b_{s+h}^{+}\right).

In local coordinates, the right derivative
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
is the slope of the extremal outward direction
\xi_{s+h}^{+}
,
which is by definition perpendicular to the vector
x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{+}
. Since
y_{s+h}^{+}
is a contributor and thus in
E
, it must by definition lie outside the
\varepsilon
-radius ball
B_{\varepsilon}(x_{s})
around the boundary point
x_{s}\in J
. One can therefore obtain a lower bound for the right derivative
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
by considering how far one could rotate the contributor
y_{s+h}^{+}
clockwise around the point
x_{s+h}
before it enters the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(x_{s})
.
This would happen at the point
y_{s+h}^{*}
, whose distance from both
x_{s}
and
x_{s+h}
equals
\varepsilon
, see Figure
9.2
. These three points thus form an isosceles triangle whose base is the line segment

(9.17)

S:=\big\{(1-\varphi)x_{s}+\varphi x_{s+h}\,:\,\varphi\in[0,1]\big\}.

Consequently, the orthogonal projection of the apex
y_{s+h}^{*}
of this triangle onto the line segment
S
lands on the mid-point
x_{s+h}^{*}:=(x_{s}+x_{s+h})/2
of
S
. Together, the points
x_{s+h}^{*}
,
y_{s+h}^{*}
and
x_{s+h}
in turn form a right triangle
\Omega
, whose legs
x_{s+h}-x_{s+h}^{*}
and
x_{s+h}^{*}-y_{s+h}^{*}
satisfy

\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s+h}^{*}\right\rVert=P(T)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\left\lVert x_{s+h}^{*}-y_{s+h}^{*}\right\rVert=A(T).

Figure 9.2
.

An illustration of the geometric components in the proof of Lemma
9.3
. One can obtain a lower bound
k(T,h):=\tan\tau(T)
for the slope
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)=\tan\tau_{s+h}=\xi_{2}^{+}(s+h)/\xi_{1}^{+}(s+h)
by considering how far one could rotate the right extremal contributor
y_{s+h}^{+}
clockwise around the point
x_{s+h}
before it enters the ball
B_{\varepsilon}(x_{s})
. The line segment
S:=\big\{(1-\varphi)x_{s}+\varphi x_{s+h}\,:\,\varphi\in[0,1]\big\}
is marked as a dashed line. Note that the illustration here does not strictly speaking apply in the situation of Lemma
9.3
because the
\xi
-coordinate of
x_{s+h}
differs from that of
x_{s}
by more than
\varepsilon/2
. This artistic liberty was taken in order to improve readability through the increase of the relevant angles.

We use the above geometric relationships to obtain an explicit lower bound for
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
in terms of the lengths
P(T)
and
A(T)
.
As noted above,
y_{s+h}^{+}\notin B_{\varepsilon}\left(x_{s}\right)
. This implies the inequality

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert y_{s+h}^{+}-x_{s}\right\rVert^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(s+h-a_{s+h}^{+}\right)-s\right)^{2}+\left(\left(f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-b_{s+h}^{+}\right)-f^{\xi,y}(s)\right)^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle=h^{2}+\left(f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)\right)^{2}-2\left(ha_{s+h}^{+}+\left(f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)\right)b_{s+h}^{+}\right).

Writing
T:=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
, the above inequality can be expressed more concisely as

(9.18)

ha_{s+h}^{+}+Tb_{s+h}^{+}\leq(h^{2}+T^{2})/2.

Since
x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{+}=\big(a_{s+h}^{+},b_{s+h}^{+}\big)
and
x_{s+h}-x_{s}=(h,T)
, (
9.18
) is furthermore equivalent to

(9.19)

\left\langle x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{+},\frac{x_{s+h}-x_{s}}{\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s}\right\rVert}\right\rangle\leq\frac{\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s}\right\rVert}{2}=P(T).

Geometrically, inequality (
9.19
) expresses the fact that the length of the orthogonal projection of the vector
x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{+}
onto the line

L:=x_{s}+\mathrm{span}\{x_{s+h}-x_{s}\}

is at most half the distance
\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s}\right\rVert
. Note that when
\xi_{s+h}^{+}
is parallel to
L
, the scalar product on the left-hand side of (
9.19
) vanishes, and for steeper slopes it becomes negative. However, since we seek to obtain a lower bound for
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)=\tan\tau_{s+h}
, where
\tau_{s+h}
is the angle between
\xi_{s+h}^{+}
and the
\xi
-axis, it is sufficient to restrict the analysis to angles
\tau_{s+h}
that are smaller than the angle
\theta(T)
between the line
L
and the
\xi
-axis.
4
4

4

Additionally, as pointed out also in the proof of Theorem
7
, the definition of a local boundary representation imposes an upper bound on the angle
\tau_{s+h}
between the extremal outward direction
\xi_{s+h}^{+}
and the
\xi
-axis, since the extremal contributor
y_{s+h}^{+}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s+h})
lies for all
s,h
within the ball
B_{\varepsilon/2}(y)
, where
y\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x)
.

Consider now the vector
x_{s+h}^{+}-y_{s+h}^{+}
, where
x_{s+h}^{+}:=\mathrm{proj}_{L}\left(y_{s+h}^{+}\right)
is the orthogonal projection of
y_{s+h}^{+}
onto the line
L
. It follows from (
9.19
) that

\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s+h}^{+}\right\rVert\leq P(T),

and consequently

\left\lVert x_{s+h}^{+}-y_{s+h}^{+}\right\rVert^{2}=\varepsilon^{2}-\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s+h}^{+}\right\rVert^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{2}-\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s+h}^{*}\right\rVert^{2}=A^{2}(T).

This implies that the angle
\alpha_{s+h}
at
y_{s+h}^{+}
between the vectors
x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{+}
and
x_{s+h}^{+}-y_{s+h}^{+}
satisfies

(9.20)

\tan\alpha_{s+h}=\frac{\left\lVert x_{s+h}-x_{s+h}^{+}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x_{s+h}^{+}-y_{s+h}^{+}\right\rVert}\leq\frac{P(T)}{A(T)}=\tan\alpha(T),

where
\alpha(T)
is the angle at
y_{s+h}^{*}
between the vectors
x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{*}
and
x_{s+h}^{*}-y_{s+h}^{*}
. Since the vectors
x_{s+h}-y_{s+h}^{+}
and
\xi_{s+h}^{+}
are perpendicular, it furthermore follows that

(9.21)

\tau_{s+h}=\theta(T)-\alpha_{s+h}.

This, together with (
9.20
), leads to the lower bound

(9.22)

\displaystyle D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)

\displaystyle=\tan\tau_{s+h}=\tan\big(\theta(T)-\alpha_{s+h}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\tan\big(\theta(T)-\alpha(T)\big)=\frac{\tan\theta(T)-\tan\alpha(T)}{1+\tan\theta(T)\tan\alpha(T)}

\displaystyle=\frac{TA(T)-hP(T)}{hA(T)+TP(T)}=k(T,h),

where the third last equality is due to the standard formula for the tangent of a sum of angles.
∎

Intuitively, Lemma
9.3
describes how the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood geometry imposes a lower bound
k(T,h)
on the tangential direction
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
, and how this bound depends on the increment
h
and the difference
T:=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
. We show next that if the point
x_{s}
and the increment
h>0
are fixed,
k(T,h)
depends monotonically on
T
.

Lemma 9.4

(Monotonicity of the lower bound for the right derivative)
.

For a fixed
h\in[0,2\varepsilon)
and the corresponding functions

P(t):=\frac{\sqrt{h^{2}+t^{2}}}{2}\quad\textrm{and}\quad A(t):=\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-P^{2}(t)}=\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{h^{2}+t^{2}}{4}},

the lower bound function

t\mapsto k(t,h):=\frac{tA(t)-hP(t)}{hA(t)+tP(t)}

in (
9.14
) is increasing on the interval
\big(\hskip-2.84526pt-\hskip-2.27621pt\sqrt{4\varepsilon^{2}-h^{2}},\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-(\varepsilon-h)^{2}}\hskip 1.70717pt\big)
.

Proof.

Note first that
A(t)
is defined when
P(t)\leq\varepsilon
, which is equivalent to
|t|\leq\sqrt{4\varepsilon^{2}-h^{2}}
.
We compute the sign of the derivative
\frac{d}{dt}k(t,h)
for a fixed
h\geq 0
. Write

k_{1}(t):=tA(t)-hP(t)\quad\textrm{and}\quad k_{2}(t):=hA(t)+tP(t),

so that
k(t,h)=k_{1}(t)/k_{2}(t)
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}k(t,h)

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{1}^{\prime}(t)k_{2}(t)-k_{1}(t)k_{2}^{\prime}(t)}{k_{2}^{2}(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{h\left(A^{2}(t)+P^{2}(t)\right)+\left(h^{2}+t^{2}\right)\left(A^{\prime}(t)P(t)-P^{\prime}(t)A(t)\right)}{\left(hA(t)+tP(t)\right)^{2}},

where

P^{\prime}(t)=t\left(2\sqrt{h^{2}+t^{2}}\right)^{-1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad A^{\prime}(t)=-t\left(4\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{h^{2}+t^{2}}{4}}\right)^{-1}.

Thus,
\frac{d}{dt}k(t,h)>0
if and only if

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<h\left(A^{2}(t)+P^{2}(t)\right)+\left(h^{2}+t^{2}\right)\big(A^{\prime}(t)P(t)-P^{\prime}(t)A(t)\big)

\displaystyle=h\varepsilon^{2}+\left(h^{2}+t^{2}\right)\frac{-t\big(h^{2}+t^{2}\big)-4t\left(\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{h^{2}+t^{2}}{4}\right)}{8\sqrt{\left(h^{2}+t^{2}\right)\left(\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{h^{2}+t^{2}}{4}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\left(h-t\frac{\sqrt{\frac{h^{2}+t^{2}}{4}}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\frac{h^{2}+t^{2}}{4}}}\right)=\varepsilon^{2}\left(h-\frac{tP(t)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-P^{2}(t)}}\right).

A direct computation shows that this inequality is satisfied if and only if
t<\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-(\varepsilon-h)^{2}}
, where the right hand side is defined for
h\in[0,2\varepsilon]
.
∎

The next lemma provides a lower bound
\widehat{T}(s,h)
for the difference
T(s,h)=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
for each
s
and
h
.
In the proof of Proposition
9.2
below, we combine this lower bound with Lemma
9.4
and show that
k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)
is in fact a lower bound for the right derivative
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)
.

Lemma 9.5

(An explicit lower bound)
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a compact set,
\varepsilon>0
and let
x\in J\subset\partial E_{\varepsilon}
where
J
is a Jordan curve. Let
\mathcal{G}(x)
be a local boundary representation at
x
, where each
g_{\xi,y}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
satisfies
g_{\xi,y}(s)=x+s\xi+f^{\xi,y}(s)\varepsilon^{-1}(x-y)
for some continuous function
f^{\xi,y}:[0,s_{\xi,y}]\to\mathbb{R}
. In addition, let
T=T(s,h):=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
, and let

k(T,h):=\frac{TA(T)-hP(T)}{hA(T)+TP(T)}

as in (
9.14
). For each
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
, write
(a_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{+}):=\big(-\varepsilon\xi_{2}^{+}(s),\varepsilon\xi_{1}^{+}(s)\big)
, where
\xi_{s}^{+}=\big(\xi_{1}^{+}(s),\xi_{2}^{+}(s)\big)
is the right extremal outward direction at the boundary point
x_{s}=\left(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\right)
, see (
9.15
).
Then

\widehat{T}(s,h):=-b_{s}^{+}+\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\big(h+a_{s}^{+}\big)^{2}}\leq T(s,h)

for all
s,h\geq 0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
. Furthermore,
\widehat{T}=\widehat{T}(s,h)
satisfies

(9.23)

k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)=p(a_{s}^{+}+h):=\frac{-\left(a_{s}^{+}+h\right)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\left(a_{s}^{+}+h\right)^{2}}},

where
p
is the slope function in (
9.11
).

Proof.

Let
s,h\geq 0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
, and consider the corresponding boundary points
x_{s},x_{s+h}\in J
whose local coordinates are given by

(9.24)

x_{s}:=\left(s,f^{\xi,y}(s)\right),\quad x_{s+h}:=\left(s+h,f^{\xi,y}(s+h)\right).

It follows from (
9.10
) that the right extremal contributors
y_{s}^{+}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s})
and
y_{s+h}^{+}\in\Pi_{E}^{\mathrm{ext}}(x_{s+h})
satisfy

(9.25)

\displaystyle y_{s}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left(s-a_{s}^{+},f^{\xi,y}(s)-b_{s}^{+}\right),

\displaystyle y_{s+h}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left(s+h-a_{s+h}^{+},f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-b_{s+h}^{+}\right).

In particular, the boundary point
x_{s+h}
and contributor
y_{s}^{+}
satisfy

\displaystyle x_{s+h}-y_{s}^{+}

\displaystyle=\big(s+h-(s-a_{s}^{+}),f^{\xi,y}(s,h)-\big(f^{\xi,y}(s)-b_{s}^{+}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(h+a_{s}^{+},T+b_{s}^{+}\big).

This implies
\big(h+a_{s}^{+}\big)^{2}+\big(T+b_{s}^{+}\big)^{2}=\left\lVert x_{s+h}-y_{s}^{+}\right\rVert^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{2}
, since
x_{s+h}\notin B_{\varepsilon}(y_{s}^{+})
.
Rearranging the terms in this inequality gives the desired estimate

(9.26)

\widehat{T}:=-b_{s}^{+}+\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\left(a_{s}^{+}+h\right)^{2}}\leq T.

Figure 9.3
.
Dependence of the function
k(T,h)=\tan\tau(T)
on
T=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
. As
T
decreases, the corresponding point
y_{s+h}^{*}(T)
on the circumference
\partial B_{\varepsilon}(x_{s})
moves clockwise towards
y_{s}^{+}
. This results in a decrease in the slope
\tan\tau(T)
, where
\tau(T):=\theta(T)-\alpha(T)
. At the smallest possible value
\widehat{T}
, one obtains the lower bound
k(\widehat{T},h)=\tan\tau(\widehat{T})=p(a_{s}^{+}+h)
, which geometrically corresponds to the point
y_{s+h}^{*}(\widehat{T})=y_{s}^{+}
.

We now show that
k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)=p(a_{s}^{+}+h)
, where
p:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
is the slope function in (
9.11
).
It follows from the definition of
\widehat{T}
that the point
\widehat{x}_{s+h}:=\big(s+h,f^{\xi,y}(s)+\widehat{T}\big)
lies on the boundary
\partial B_{\varepsilon}\left(y_{s}^{+}\right)
. Furthermore,
\widehat{x}_{s+h}-y_{s}^{+}=\big(\widehat{a}_{s+h},\widehat{b}_{s+h}\big)
, where
5
5

5

By construction,
b_{s}^{+}
is always positive, while
a_{s}^{+}
may either be positive or negative depending on the slope
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)
, see (
9.10
).

\widehat{a}_{s+h}:=a_{s}^{+}+h,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\widehat{b}_{s+h}:=\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\left(a_{s}^{+}+h\right)^{2}}.

Now, define
z_{s+h}:=\big(s+h,f^{\xi,y}(s)-b_{s}^{+}\big)
so that
z_{s+h}
shares its
\xi
–coordinate with
\widehat{x}_{s+h}
and its
\varepsilon^{-1}(x-y)
–coordinate with
y_{s}^{+}
. Recall that for each
h
, the function
T\mapsto k\big(T,h\big)
is defined by
k\big(T,h\big)=\tan\big(\theta(T)-\alpha(T)\big)
where the
T
-dependent angles
\theta(T)
and
\alpha(T)
are defined as in the proof of Lemma
9.3
, see (
9.22
). Then

\displaystyle k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)

\displaystyle=\tan\big(\theta(\widehat{T})-\alpha(\widehat{T})\big)=\tan\tau(\widehat{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{-\widehat{a}_{s+h}}{\widehat{b}_{s+h}}=\frac{-\big(a_{s}^{+}+h\big)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-\big(a_{s}^{+}+h\big)^{2}}}

\displaystyle=p\big(a_{s}^{+}+h\big),

where
\tau(\widehat{T})
is the angle at
\widehat{x}_{s+h}
between the vectors
y_{s}^{+}-\widehat{x}_{s+h}
and
z_{s+h}-\widehat{x}_{s+h}
, see Figure
9.3
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
9.3
–
9.5
, we are now ready to prove our second main result.

Proof of Proposition
9.2
.

Let
s,h>0
with
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
and let
T:=f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)
. Define

k(T,h):=\frac{TA(T)-hP(T)}{hA(T)+TP(T)}

as in (
9.14
) and let
\widehat{T}\leq T
be the lower bound, given by Lemma
9.5
, for which
k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)=p(a_{s}^{+}+h)
. The slope function
p:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
p(s):=-s/\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-s^{2}}
as in (
9.11
).

We want to use the monotonicity of the map
T\mapsto k(T,h)
in order to establish for each
h
the inequality
k(T,h)\geq k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)
. For this, we need to show that
T
satisfies for all
h
the assumption

-\sqrt{4\varepsilon^{2}-h^{2}}<T<\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-(\varepsilon-h)^{2}}

in Lemma
9.4
. This is obtained immediately by combining the Lipschitz property
6
6

6

Note that the function
f^{\xi,y}
here corresponds to the orthonormal coordinate system
\big(\xi,\varepsilon^{-1}(x-y)\big)
, and is thus scaled by a factor of
\varepsilon
compared to the corresponding function
f^{\xi,y}
in the statement of Proposition
3.6
, see equations (
9.2
) and (
3.13
). Hence, the Lipschitz constant here is
1/\sqrt{3}
rather than
1/\sqrt{3}\varepsilon
.

|T|=|f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-f^{\xi,y}(s)|\leq h/\sqrt{3},

given by Proposition
3.6
, with the inequalities

\frac{h}{\sqrt{3}}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}-(\varepsilon-h)^{2}}<\sqrt{4\varepsilon^{2}-h^{2}},

which follow from
0\leq h\leq s_{\xi,y}\leq\varepsilon/2
. The results in Lemmas
9.3
–
9.5
thus imply that

(9.27)

D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)\geq k(T,h)\geq k\big(\widehat{T},h\big)=p(a_{s}^{+}+h)

whenever
s,h\geq 0
and
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
. Furthermore, according to (
9.11
), the slope of
f^{\xi,y}
at
s
satisfies
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)=p\left(a_{s}^{+}\right)
, which together with (
9.27
) implies

(9.28)

D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)\geq p(a_{s}^{+}+h)-p(a_{s}^{+}).

We now establish this inequality also for the left derivatives
D^{-}f^{\xi,y}
.
Recall from (
9.11
) that

D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s)=\frac{\xi_{2}^{-}(s)}{\xi_{1}^{-}(s)}

for all
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
, where
\xi_{s}^{-}=\big(\xi_{1}^{-}(s),\xi_{2}^{-}(s)\big)\in\Xi_{x_{s}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
is the left extremal outward direction at
x_{s}
. Consider a sequence
(x_{s_{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset J
where
s_{n}\to s
from below, so that
x_{s_{n}}\to x_{s}
and

(9.29)

\frac{x_{s_{n}}-x_{s}}{\left\lVert x_{s_{n}}-x_{s}\right\rVert}\to\xi_{s}^{-}.

It follows from Lemma
2.13
(ii)(b) that the right extremal outward directions
\xi_{s_{n}}^{+}\in\Xi_{x_{s_{n}}}^{\mathrm{ext}}(E_{\varepsilon})
satisfy
\xi_{s_{n}}^{+}\to-\xi_{s}^{-}
as
n\to\infty
. Since
D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})=\xi_{2}^{+}(s_{n})/\xi_{1}^{+}(s_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, this implies

(9.30)

\lim_{n\to\infty}D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\xi_{2}^{+}(s_{n})}{\xi_{1}^{+}(s_{n})}=\frac{-\xi_{2}^{-}(s)}{-\xi_{1}^{-}(s)}=D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s).

This limit is independent of the choice of the sequence
(s_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
in (
9.29
). Since the slope function
p
is continuous on
[0,\varepsilon/2]
, it follows from (
9.30
) and (
9.27
) that for all
s,h\geq 0
and
0\leq s+h\leq s_{\xi,y}
,

(9.31)

\displaystyle D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varphi\to 0-}D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s+h+\varphi)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\varphi\to 0-}p(a_{s}^{+}+h+\varphi)

\displaystyle=p(a_{s}^{+}+h).

Due to the characterisation of the sets of extremal outward directions given in Proposition
2.12
, there can be no cusp singularities on the boundary
J
. Given the relationship (
9.11
) between extremal outward directions and the one-sided derivatives, this implies that

D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s)\leq D^{+}f^{\xi,y}(s)=p(a_{s}^{+})

for all
s\in[0,s_{\xi,y}]
. Combining this inequality with (
9.27
) and (
9.31
) yields the analogue of (
9.28
) for the left derivatives,

(9.32)

D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s+h)-D^{-}f^{\xi,y}(s)\geq p(a_{s}^{+}+h)-p(a_{s}^{+}).

We conclude the proof by obtaining a lower bound for the difference
p(a_{s}^{+}+h)-p(a_{s}^{+})
. A direct calculation shows that the derivative of
p
is given by

p^{\prime}(s)=\frac{p(s)+p^{3}(s)}{s}.

Since
p
is an odd function, it follows that
p^{\prime}
is even and non-positive. Then

p^{\prime}_{\mathrm{min}}:=\min_{|s|\leq s_{\xi,y}}p^{\prime}(s)\geq\min_{|s|\leq\varepsilon/2}p^{\prime}(s)=p^{\prime}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)=-\frac{8}{3\sqrt{3}\varepsilon},

which implies

\displaystyle p(a_{s}^{+}+h)-p(a_{s}^{+})

\displaystyle=\int_{a_{s}^{+}}^{a_{s}^{+}+h}p^{\prime}(s)ds\geq\int_{a_{s}^{+}}^{a_{s}^{+}+h}p^{\prime}_{\mathrm{min}}ds\geq-\frac{8h}{3\sqrt{3}\varepsilon}.

The result now follows from (
9.28
) and (
9.32
).
∎
